(JDI) has drawn interest in the sale of its Mobara plant (fab), which halted operations this March, not only from Micron of the United States and several data center operators but also from a semiconductor substrate maker, according to sources on the 8th.  The JDI Mobara plant previously produced sixth generation liquid crystal displays (LCD) and organic light emitting diodes (OLED). It also mass produced OLED panels for the Apple Watch until the second half of 2025. As JDI had announced in the first quarter of 2025, it halted operations at Mobara in March 2026.  Industry source A said, "Micron and several data center operators have submitted letters of intent to JDI to acquire the Mobara plant." Another source B said, "Micron and a Korean semiconductor substrate maker, among others, are in talks with JDI over purchasing the Mobara plant." At its quarterly earnings announcement in early August, JDI stated that it was in discussions with multiple companies regarding the sale of the plant.  The reason Micron, data center operators, and semiconductor substrate makers are interested in the Mobara plant comes down to time. Building a new plant from scratch and securing government permits would take years before mass production could begin. Buying the Mobara plant shortens that timeline accordingly.  Because Mobara previously produced sixth generation LCD and OLED panels, its cleanroom cleanliness levels make it unsuitable for semiconductor front end processing, but the site can be used as a data center or as a back end packaging plant for products such as glass substrates. That is why Micron, data center operators, and substrate makers are interested. The sale price for the plant is currently reported to be under discussion at around KRW 500 billion, though the figure could change depending on circumstances.  OLEDWorks, a US OLED company working with JDI, is reported to be interested not in the Mobara plant but in the 4.5 generation Ishikawa plant. Ishikawa has a line capable of backplane (BP) processing. For OLEDWorks, securing a plant that can reliably handle BP processing going forward is a necessity.  JDI is currently considering selling its plants, including Mobara and Ishikawa, separately to different buyers. If each of these deals goes through, the ownership of JDI's plants would change hands individually.  JDI was formed in 2012 under the lead of the Japanese government (Innovation Network Corporation of Japan) by consolidating the LCD businesses of Sony, Toshiba, and Hitachi. It subsequently failed to respond to market shifts such as the growing adoption of OLED in smartphones and fell behind Korean and Chinese panel makers in competition. In November 2024, JDI unveiled a new catchphrase, "Beyond Display." Whereas the old JDI consisted solely of displays, the company said the new JDI would comprise displays, sensors, artificial intelligence (AI) data centers, and advanced semiconductor packaging.  Meanwhile, most of the equipment related to eLEAP, the technology JDI had developed at the Mobara plant, was purchased by China's HKC. eLEAP patterns red, green, and blue (RGB) OLED subpixels through a photolithography process without using a fine metal mask (FMM). Its viability for mass production has yet to be verified. The technology is similar in principle to FLiPP, which LG Display unveiled at IMID this August.  The appeal of JDI's eLEAP patents is limited. This is because JDI is reported to have sold licenses (rights of use) for the patents to Applied Materials (AMAT), which makes the deposition equipment required to implement eLEAP, and others. JDI sells the licenses to AMAT and others and collects royalties, which means a third party that buys the patents from JDI cannot use them exclusively. Several panel makers in Korea and abroad are developing eLEAP type technology, but there is little incentive to purchase the JDI patents.  $MU | 상대: Meta, Applied Materials | - | ⚠️ 공급망속보 | `[C2]` |

| **L3 컴퓨팅/가속기** | **Intel** (INTC 🇺🇸) | 2026-09-14 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Samsung Electro-Mechanics and Qualcomm Form 2.1D Packaging Alliance, Jointly Developing an "Organic Bridge" That Bypasses Intel's EMIB  Samsung Electro-Mechanics is teaming up with Qualcomm to develop "organic bridge" technology for advanced packaging of artificial intelligence (AI) chips. Beyond Qualcomm, Samsung Electro-Mechanics is also co-developing organic bridge technology with several other customers in parallel.  An organic bridge is similar to the silicon bridge used in Intel's EMIB (Embedded Multi-die Interconnect Bridge) packaging, but it uses organic materials, the kind commonly found in printed circuit boards (PCBs), instead of silicon.  According to industry sources on the 14th, Samsung Electro-Mechanics is jointly developing organic bridge technology with Qualcomm for use in 2.1D packaging. Development and validation work has been under way for more than a year. The evaluation involves embedding an organic bridge into a flip-chip ball grid array (FC-BGA) substrate that connects Qualcomm chips, and then assessing the substrate's performance and reliability.  Qualcomm is understood to be considering the organic bridge for large multi-die chips for data centers, among other applications. In October 2024, the company filed a patent for an "Interconnect Bridge" technology, in which a separately fabricated organic material based bridge is embedded in a package substrate to connect multiple dies. In March this year, Qualcomm also hired organic bridge personnel in Korea.  An organic bridge is a component that connects multiple semiconductor dies integrated in a single package. It can be thought of as an ultra small substrate with ultra fine circuitry. It is made by forming multiple layers of fine circuits on an organic dielectric using a redistribution layer (RDL) process. Because it enables finer wiring than the build-up circuitry of a conventional FC-BGA, it can carry more signals across a smaller area. Its role is to raise input/output (I/O) density and bandwidth between dies such as graphics processing units (GPUs) and high bandwidth memory (HBM). It serves a similar function to the silicon bridge used in Intel's EMIB, but it is built on organic material using substrate manufacturing processes rather than semiconductor processes.  Samsung Electro-Mechanics produces the FC-BGA substrate with a recess (cavity) formed at the location where the organic bridge is embedded. For the organic bridge itself, the company is weighing two options: in house production and external sourcing. It produces small quantities of organic bridges for evaluation on its own pilot line, and samples are also made at DSRJ (Device Solutions R&D Japan), the Japanese R&D organization under the Samsung Advanced Institute of Technology (SAIT). The company is also evaluating an approach in which organic bridges manufactured by outside partners are supplied and embedded into its FC-BGA substrates.  In addition to Qualcomm, Samsung Electro-Mechanics is developing organic bridge based 2.1D package substrates with multiple other customers. The strategy is to bypass Intel's EMIB packaging IP. Intel developed EMIB to address the cost and productivity issues of silicon interposers, placing small bridges only where high density connections are required. Intel has applied the technology in mass production since 2017 and has secured the related IP. Several big tech companies are now looking at organic bridges as an alternative to silicon bridges.  Samsung Electro-Mechanics' organic bridge based 2.1D package substrate technology embeds an organic bridge with five to seven redistribution layers (RDL) of circuitry into an FC-BGA. The goal is to shrink the organic bridge's line width and spacing to 1.5 to 2 micrometers (㎛) each, with via sizes of 4 to 5 ㎛. Die to die pattern density is targeted at 500 to 1,000 per millimeter (mm).  Collaboration with Samsung Foundry is also drawing attention. Once Samsung Electro-Mechanics manufactures the organic bridge based FC-BGA, Samsung Foundry evaluates the package's performance and reliability. Samsung Foundry is developing advanced packaging technologies including "2.3D Cube-E," which uses a silicon bridge, and "2.3D Cube-R," which is based on an organic RDL interposer. Samsung Electro-Mechanics' organic bridge substrate is expected to lead to an expansion of Samsung Foundry's packaging services.  $QCOM $INTC | 상대: ASE, Samsung | 제품: TPU, HBM | - | 💬 분석가전망 |

| **L3 컴퓨팅/가속기** | **Intel** (INTC 🇺🇸) | 2026-09-15 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): RT @teortaxesTex: Full text (translated by Astra-xhigh, I'm out of everything else):   I Have No Choice but to Bury My Talent in Yesterday  A few days ago, DeepSeek v4.1 was released, raising the ceiling of what small models can do by yet another notch.  AI has advanced far faster than anyone expected. From the earliest version of ChatGPT, which could do little more than stumble through conversations like a child learning to speak and had a context window of only a few thousand tokens, to reasoning-capable models such as OpenAI o1, DeepSeek R1, and Kimi K1.5 Thinking, took only two short years. From reasoning models to the agents we have today—able to work fluidly with all kinds of tool harnesses, execute commands, and complete complex tasks—has taken only another year and a half.  It is hard to imagine what AI will look like another one, two, or three years from now: how powerful it will be, whether it will already have acquired the ability to improve itself, and how deeply it will have spread into areas such as embodied intelligence.  AI Is Getting Better and Better at Writing Kernels  AI has been advancing just as quickly in my own field: the design and implementation of high-performance kernels.  In the space of only a year, it has gone from being a little assistant that could help me look up documentation, read code, and find bugs to something approaching a kernel expert in its own right: capable of reading CUDA, PTX, and SASS code independently, using specialized tools to analyze the stalls associated with individual instructions, and then optimizing kernels on its own.  I believe that before long, it will also be able to design kernel schedules independently, evaluate the performance of different scheduling strategies, implement them, and optimize the result.  Of course I am proud of DeepSeek v4.1’s success. After all, I wrote its main Attention kernels [1], and the fact that the model performs so well is also, in a sense, a validation of my work.  But the times keep moving forward, and no one can stop technological progress. I know very well that in another six months or a year, the kernels written by AI will probably be every bit as good as mine—and perhaps better.  AI can reason at 300 tokens a second, type out a command in half a second, and produce a piece of code in twenty seconds. I cannot. AI can keep increasing its model depth, reasoning effort, tool-call budget—the frequency with which it interacts with its environment—and even its degree of parallelism. I cannot.  Humanity has never shown much hesitation when it comes to destroying itself.  So why, when I know perfectly well that “the better the kernels I write, the faster our new models will train and run inference; the faster the models improve, the sooner I myself will be replaced,” do I still do everything I can to optimize them?  Partly because writing kernels is like playing a game to me. I get an enormous amount of pleasure from it. Whenever I invent a new technique, or see one of my kernels become faster, the excitement I feel is no less intense than what a speedrunner feels after breaking their own record. And when I see one of my kernels dramatically outperform the hardware vendor’s official implementation, I feel an equally powerful sense of pride.  But there is a more important reason.  Even if I simply gave up and started coasting—or deliberately put obstacles in the way to slow down model training—other companies’ models would continue advancing as usual, and in the end they would make me obsolete just the same.  “Of course I would rather not be swept away by the revolution. But if I have to be, then I would rather be the one who revolutionizes myself.”  When everyone is this determined to engineer their own obsolescence, I have little choice but to join this brutal arms race.  And What About Me?  When the day really comes that AI is better at writing kernels than I am, what will happen to me then?  My own judgment is this: I probably will not lose my job, but I will have to change what I do.  I should still be able to make a living. But I may no longer have the chance to do the work I once loved.  I once came to a conclusion about the pace of change and my own place in the future. The world is changing so quickly—the development of AI above is a perfect example—that I have no way at all to predict what things will look like five or ten years from now. But whatever happens, I believe that with my breadth of vision, judgment, initiative, and intelligence, I will be able to keep a seat at the table and find my way back to the leading edge of the times.  But that conclusion can only reassure me that I will not become unemployed. It cannot reassure me that I will never have to change professions. If anything, it tells me that changing professions may be precisely how I avoid unemployment.  And what does changing professions mean?  It means giving up the field of kernel design, implementation, and optimization that I have spent so long cultivating and have come to love so deeply, and instead becoming a “mech pilot” for AI agents.  Before, three things were largely aligned: what interested me, what I was good at, and what industry needed. Now AI has taken the thing I am good at and become even better at it. At the same time, industry demand has drifted from “people who can write high-performance kernels” to “people who can use AI to produce high-performance kernels faster.”  To keep up with what industry needs, I will inevitably have to leave behind the direction I once loved and move into some unknown new one.  I believe that with my understanding of engineering, of the requirements of higher-level models, and of low-level hardware, I will still be able to produce high-quality kernels efficiently. I also know that I may come to love this new direction.  Or I may not.  But there is something genuinely painful about having the thing you love taken away from you.  That quiet contentment of sitting at my workstation, settling in, and spending an entire afternoon writing kernels may sing its swan song this summer.  I have no choice but to bury my talent in yesterday and become a mech pilot.  There are more gears in my hands now, but fewer rhythms in my heart.  An analogy might make this easier to picture.  Suppose you are a master knitter. You are especially skilled at weaving intricate patterns and matching different colors. The sweaters you make are durable and beautifully patterned, and wealthy people from all the surrounding towns and villages come to ask you to make sweaters for them. You make a good living from it.  And you genuinely love the work itself. You love sitting by the window, brewing a pot of tea, looking out at the green hills, clear water, cattle and sheep, and wisps of cooking smoke in the distance, and quietly spending an afternoon knitting.  Then one day, someone invents a miraculous machine. Give it yarn and a pattern, and it can automatically knit the sweater for you. The quality and texture are every bit as good as what you could make by hand, and it works far faster than you ever could.  You know perfectly well that your peers can use this machine to reach, effortlessly, the level you once spent years attaining. So you have no choice but to use it as well.  You also know that with the twenty years of knitting experience you have accumulated, even once everyone has access to the same machine, you will still be able to produce better sweaters, faster, than your peers.  But the pleasure of sitting by the window listening to the rain, guiding needle and thread, and letting the hours pass slowly has, in the end, been crushed beneath the roar of the machine.  I know there is something deeply helpless about all of this, but there is no real way around it. I can probably keep my livelihood, but I will most likely have to give up an old love.  I am the sort of person who keeps reason and emotion fairly compartmentalized. When something needs to be handled rationally, I can be very rational. But I also have a sentimental side.  I remember that when I moved out of an apartment I had lived in for a year, I cried hard because I could not bear to part with all the memories tied to that place.  Saying goodbye today to the age when kernels were written by hand and optimized in the human mind is undoubtedly more painful still.  I do not know whether any readers have felt something similar.  But I suppose there is no other way for this to go.  And What About Everyone Else?  As AI continues to improve, I also find myself worried about a few questions:  Are students today increasingly likely to use AI to do their assignments, especially hands-on work such as labs? Imagine having two choices in front of you. One is to spend eight miserable hours struggling through a lab and perhaps not even get full marks. The other is to launch an AI model, spend a few cents and a few minutes, and have it write code that earns full marks for you. Which one are most students going to choose?  The point above may leave large numbers of students with seriously underdeveloped engineering ability: the ability to organize code, build systems, anticipate future needs and design for them in advance, create good abstractions, and so on. As AI becomes more capable, will those “engineering skills” still be necessary? Will they gradually become obsolete, the way fluency in handwritten x86 assembly largely has? Or will they remain permanently valuable, like understanding the entire computing stack from software to systems to hardware? If it is the latter, then we may be in trouble. Put AI in the hands of someone with poor engineering judgment, and they can now produce mountains of terrible code several times faster than before, burying all kinds of hidden problems inside systems and making the world even more of a ramshackle operation held together by improvisation.  In the society of the future, will power matter more than technical ability or intelligence?  Perhaps these are questions that only the times themselves can answer.  Conclusion  As AI develops, the society of the future may be pulled toward one of two extremes: communism or Cyberpunk 2077.  In the former, productive capacity is liberated on an enormous scale, and people’s standard of living rises substantially. (I’ll leave it at that, or I’m afraid this might not make it past moderation.)  In the latter, a handful of technology companies control most of society’s resources. Only a tiny number of people have access to the most advanced AI and other technologies and are able to achieve something approaching “mechanical ascension,” while most people are left with only weak, second-rate AI.  Moving from one social class to another would become harder and harder: you would first need access to the strongest AI in order to climb the class ladder, creating a self-reinforcing trap.  Suppose Anthropic were to retain control of the most advanced AI in the world indefinitely.  Which way do you think society would go—communism or 2077?  Take a guess.  That is why I still believe that frontier intelligence should be made available to everyone openly and affordably.  I do not trust Anthropic or OpenAI to do that. In particular, I do not want Anthropic to control the world’s most advanced artificial intelligence or AGI. To put it dramatically, I think the stakes would be comparable to Hitler obtaining the atomic bomb before the Allies did.  That is also why I chose to stay at DeepSeek, and why I have continued to stay.  We work on AI that is powerful, fast, and accessible to everyone, and we open-source it. Perhaps that can pull the world at least a little farther away from the 2077 end of the spectrum.  I hope the world we are heading into turns out all right.  May all that is good and beautiful endure.  [1] By “main Attention,” I mean only MQA attention with head dim = 512. This does not include the indexer used to select the top-k important tokens. That part was written by other colleagues—who are every bit as skilled—together with their AI agents.  -----  Original:  我不得不把才华埋葬在昨天  前几天，DeepSeek v4.1 发布了，将小模型能力的高度又向上推进了一个档次。 AI 发展的速度远远超过了所有人的预期。从那个只会咿呀学语地聊天、上下文长度只有几千 token 的初版 ChatGPT，到具有推理能力的 OpenAI o1、DeepSeek R1 与 Kimi K1.5 Thinking，只不过短短两年；从推理模型到如今能够流畅地在各类 harness 工具中执行命令、完成复杂任务的智能体，也不过一年半。很难想象，倘若再等上一年、两年、三年，彼时的 AI 会成为什么样子，会有多么强大，会不会已经具备了自我进化的能力，并深度渗透进了具身智能等领域。 AI 越来越会写算子了 AI 在我所从事的算子设计、编写这一领域同样进步飞速，在短短一年的时间内，他已经从一个只能帮我查查文档、读读代码、找找 bug 的小助手，蜕变成了一位能够独立阅读 CUDA、PTX 与 SASS 编码、通过专业工具分析每条指令的停顿时间、进而独立优化算子的算子大师。相信在不久的未来，它也能拥有自己独立设计算子调度、评估不同调度方案的性能、将其实现并优化的能力。 我当然为 DeepSeek v4.1 的成功而骄傲 —— 毕竟它的主 Attention 算子都是我写的 [1]，它的优秀正是对我的算子的一份肯定。但是，时代的车轮滚滚向前，技术的发展无人能挡。我很清楚，再过上半年或者一年，AI 写的算子大概率就会和我写得同样优秀，甚至将我超越。AI 能一秒思考 300 个 token、半秒敲出一行命令、二十秒写完一份代码，而我不行；AI 能在模型深度、思考强度、工具调用量（和环境交互的频率）、甚至并行度等方面都能不断提升，而我不能。 人类在毁灭自己这件事情上，自古以来都表现得毫不犹豫。为什么在明知“我算子写得越好，我们的新模型的训练、推理速度就会越快，模型能力进步就会更快，我就会更早地被取代”的情况下，我仍然选择尽力优化算子呢？一方面确实是因为写算子对我来说就像打游戏一样，能为我提供极大的快感。我在发明了一种新技术、或者看到自己算子的性能上升的那一刻，心中的激动程度不亚于游戏的速通玩家打破了自己过往的记录。同时，当看到自己的算子的性能远超厂商官方的算子时，我心中也会萌生极大的自豪感。但除此之外，一个更重要的原因是，哪怕我就此“摆烂”甚至故意下绊子耽误模型训练，其它家的模型也会照常发展并最终将我照杀不误。“我当然希望自己不要被革命，但如果非被革命不可的话，我希望革我自己命的人是我自己”。在大家都这么执着于毁灭自己的时候，我也不得不加入这场残酷的军备竞赛。 那我呢 等到 AI 写算子的水平真的高于我的那天，届时的我会怎么样呢？ 我的判断是：我不至于会“失业”，但必须要“转业”。我的饭碗尚且能保住，但这可能会导致我再也没机会从事那份我曾热爱过的工作。 我曾经对时代的变化与我个人在未来的处境做出过一个判断：由于时代变化真的太快（上文的 AI 发展就是一个很好的例子），我完全无法预知五年、十年后会发生什么，但不论如何，我相信凭借着自己的眼界、判断力、主观能动性与智力，留在时代的牌桌上，并重新立于时代的潮头。但是，这个判断只能保证我不会“失业”，而无法保证我不需要“转业”，倒不如说这个判断鼓励我通过转业来避免失业。 那转业代表什么呢？它代表着我需要放弃我深耕已久并充满热爱的算子设计、编写、优化领域，转而去做 Agent 的“机甲驾驶员”。在之前，我的兴趣、我所擅长的、以及工业界所需要的，三者是基本对齐的；而现在，AI 让我所擅长的变成了它更擅长的，也让工业界的需求从“会写高性能算子的人”漂移到了“能用 AI 更快地产出高性能算子的人”。为了适应工业界的需求，我势必要放弃之前那个我热爱的方向，转向一个未知的新方向。我相信我能凭借着自己对于工程学、上层模型需求和底层硬件的理解，继续高质量、高效率地产出算子，我也知道我可能会热爱这个新方向（也可能不会），但被夺走热爱的感觉，确实不太好受。那份坐在工位上静心写上一下午算子的清欢，可能会在这个夏天成为绝唱。我不得不把才华埋葬在昨天，去做一位机甲驾驶员。我的手中多了些齿轮，但心中少了些节拍。 可以打个形象的比方：你精通织毛衣技术，尤其擅长各种图案的织造与各色色彩的搭配。你所织出的毛衣质量过硬且花纹美观，十里八乡的富人都来请你为他们织毛衣，你借此赚到了不少钱。同时，你十分享受着那种坐在窗边，沏一壶清茶，望着窗外的青山、绿水、牛羊与炊烟，静静地织上一下午毛衣的感觉。但有一天，有人发明出了一台神奇的机器，只需提供毛线与图案，便可自动织出毛衣，质量与纹理都不亚于你亲手织造的，且速度远快于你。你很清楚，你的同行可以凭着这台机器轻松达到你曾经的水平，因此你不得不也去用它。你也知道，凭借着你过去二十年攒下的织毛衣技术，哪怕大家都有机器，你织毛衣的速度与质量也还能超过同行。但那份临窗听雨、引针穿线、慢度光阴的意趣，终究还是被机器的轰鸣碾碎了。 我知道这很无奈，但没办法。饭碗可以保住，但旧日的热爱大概率是要放弃的。我是一个理性和感性分离得比较开的人，在需要用理性处理问题时可以很理性，但有时也会表现出感性的一面。我记得我在搬离住了一年的出租屋时，还大哭了一场，舍不得和过去的记忆分别。今天和之前那个手写算子、人脑优化的时代告别，无疑比这更加残酷。 不知道有没有读者有类似的感受，但我想这事儿也只能这样了。 那人们呢 在 AI 不断进步的同时，我也对一些问题表示担忧： 现在的学生是不是大概率会更倾向于使用 AI 完成作业，特别是偏向于实践的各种 Lab？想象一下，如果面前有两个选择，一个是苦哈哈地用八小时时间完成一个 Lab，或许还拿不到满分；另一个则是启动 AI 模型，用几毛钱的成本、几分钟的时间，直接让 AI 编写满分代码，那大部分学生会选择哪个呢？ 上面一点会导致大量学生的工程能力严重不足，包括组织代码的能力、构建系统的能力、思考未来潜在需求并提前在设计上应对的能力、抽象的能力等等。那么在 AI 能力不断变强的背景下，这部分“工程能力”是否还是必须的呢？这些工程能力是会向旧日的“熟练编写 x86 汇编”的能力那样逐渐被时代抛弃，还是会像“理解从软件到系统再到硬件的整套计算机系统”的能力那样永远具有价值？如果是后者的话，那就危险了 —— 一个工程能力很差的人，在搭配上 AI 后，产出屎山的效率可以达到先前的数倍，进而给系统埋下各式祸患，让这个世界变得更加草台。 在未来社会中，权力（power）是不是会比技术或智商更加重要？ 这些问题，或许就需要时代本身来回答了。 结语 伴随着 AI 的发展，未来的社会可能会趋向于两个极端：共产主义与赛博朋克 2077。在前者中，生产力得到极大的解放，人们的生活水平有了明显的提高（就写这些吧不然我怕过不了审）；而在后者中，少数科技公司控制着大部分资源，只有极少数人能够使用最先进的 AI 和各式科技，获得接近“机械飞升”的效果，大部分人则只能用上很孱弱的 AI。阶层跨越将越来越难实现：你得先有最强的 AI，才能跨越阶层，形成了一种死循环。 你猜猜如果 Anthropic 公司永远掌握着这个世界上最先进的 AI，未来社会是会变成共产主义还是 2077 呢？你猜？ 所以，我还是相信，最前沿的智能应该以一种开放、廉价的方式，供应给所有人。我不信任 Anthropic 或者 OpenAI 能这样做，特别是不希望 Anthropic 掌握最先进的人工智能或 AGI，夸张点说其严重性不亚于让希特勒先于盟军掌握原子弹技术。这也是为什么我选择并坚持留在了 DeepSeek：我们研究强大、快速、普惠的人工智能并将其开源，或许能把世界从 2077 那端拉回来一些。 愿未来的世界一切安好。May all the beauty be blessed. | **L3 컴퓨팅/가속기** | **Intel** (INTC 🇺🇸) | 2026-09-18 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): SK hynix Subsidiary Solidigm Pursues Its First U.S. NAND Fab  Solidigm, the NAND subsidiary of SK hynix, is reviewing the construction of its first NAND mass production line in the United States, it was learned on the 19th. The company is currently working through details such as the fab site and the timing of the investment.  With NAND demand surging on the expansion of AI infrastructure buildouts, the move is being read as a strategy to strengthen cooperation with customers in the U.S. It also lines up with the push to expand U.S. AI investment that SK Chairman Chey Tae won has been leading at the group level.  Solidigm is the U.S. entity SK hynix established when it acquired Intel's NAND flash business at the end of 2021. The total deal size was $9 billion (about 10 trillion won at the exchange rate of the time). Solidigm operates Fab 1 in Dalian, China, and began capital spending on Dalian Fab 2 in the third quarter of this year. In the U.S. it has only its headquarters and an R&D center, with no production base.  The region Solidigm is considering for its first U.S. production base is the East Coast. The company is understood to be discussing the details, including site selection, product mix and the timing of mass production.  A person familiar with the matter said, "Solidigm is pursuing a local mass production line to respond to NAND demand for AI servers from U.S. big tech companies," adding that "it will firm up its investment plan based on long term agreement (LTA) negotiations with customers."  Solidigm has so far focused on developing quad level cell (QLC) NAND for AI data centers. QLC stores four bits in a single cell, the smallest unit of data storage. Compared with other approaches such as triple level cell (TLC), which stores three bits, it is better suited to building high capacity SSDs.  Orders for high capacity NAND have been piling up in particular as the NAND capacity required by next generation AI platforms such as Nvidia's Blackwell and Vera Rubin climbs sharply. According to market research firm TrendForce, NAND revenue at SK hynix (including Solidigm) reached $14.2729 billion (about 19.8 trillion won) in the second quarter of this year, up 89.5% from the previous quarter.  Solidigm's U.S. investment fits with the broader expansion of U.S. investment that SK Group is pursuing. Following the listing of SK hynix American depositary receipts (ADR) and the construction of an advanced packaging fab in the U.S., SK Group is also leaving the door open to building an additional memory production plant. Solidigm itself is preparing an initial public offering (IPO) on the Nasdaq.  At an event in New York in July marking the SK hynix ADR listing, Chairman Chey said, "In addition to the advanced packaging facility in Indiana, we are reviewing further investments," and "if conditions such as power, water, workforce and supply chain are in place, building a memory production plant is also possible."  More recently, talk of SK hynix and Intel cooperating on memory production in the U.S. has also surfaced through foreign media and industry sources. It will take time before anything is finalized, but the industry view is that it is feasible, given that U.S. demand for AI memory such as high bandwidth memory (HBM) is expected to keep growing over the medium to long term.  Regarding its U.S. capital investment plans, a Solidigm official said, "We are reviewing various options to strengthen our business competitiveness, but nothing has been decided."  $SKHY | 상대: NVIDIA, ASE | 제품: Blackwell B200, Rubin R100 | 금액: $9 | 💬 분석가전망 |

| **L3 컴퓨팅/가속기** | **Intel** (INTC 🇺🇸) | 2026-10-02 | [𝕏 Radar] Todd Gordon(@TFMkts): RT @RaoulGMI: China added more solar last year than the entire worlds standing stock of solar.   This is Wright's Law doing what it always does... every time cumulative production doubles, the cost falls by a fixed percentage. Solar has sat on that curve for decades. China just poured scale into it.  So the cost of creating energy is collapsing. Meanwhile compute costs are collapsing too. | **L3 컴퓨팅/가속기** | **Intel** (INTC 🇺🇸) | 2026-10-02 | [𝕏 Radar] Chamath Palihapitiya(@chamath): We'll be spending a lot more time trying to understand the outputs of language models. A few thoughts, tips & tricks:  Writing. Something I've had success with: Ask your LLM to explain something in ASD-STE100, it's a controlled language specification originally developed for aerospace maintenance documentation. LLMs well-versed in this language and it comes with heavy constraints on clean writing style that I often find a lot more readable. Sometimes I've tried to soften it a bit e.g. ask for "80% of the way to ASD-STE100" because the spec is quite stringent. But even better:  Diagrams / images. Instead of writing, ask your LLM to create a diagram. These can be a lot easier to process, parse, and understand. But even better:  Web pages. Ask for output "in HTML" to get a beautiful, interactive webpage. LLMs are getting really good at frontend and can create beautiful experiences, animations, etc. But even better:  Explainer videos. The output format I am most bullish on is fully custom / bespoke explainer videos generated on any arbitrary topic. Experiment with things like "Create a 3b1b style video explainer on X. Use my ElevenLabs API key for audio narration". (you'd need an API key for the latter or you can ask your LLM to find you decent free alternatives that use your local compute). This is actually starting to work!  In summary: - As LLMs get better, they will do more and more of the legwork autonomously, and a lot more of our work will rise up the abstractions into oversight and understanding. - Luckily, LLMs can help here too because as intelligence and code are increasingly abundant, you can ask for large, custom, discardable software artifacts (e.g. web apps, video explainers) that would have never made sense to create before. Push the boundaries here and you'll be surprised. | 제품: TPU | - | 💬 트위터 | `[C1]` |

Un pays de 6 millions d'habitants tenu par deux gangs, la MS-13 et Barrio 18, avec des dizaines de milliers de membres. Ils contrôlent des quartiers entiers. Tu franchis la mauvaise frontière invisible entre deux territoires, tu meurs. Chaque commerce paie la "renta". Aujourd'hui, les gens sortent le soir, les terrasses sont pleines, les surfeurs débarquent du monde entier, le tourisme explose, la diaspora revient. Le Salvador est devenu un petit paradis.  Moi, je suis très optimiste sur la France. On a la meilleure culture du monde. Les meilleures infrastructures. Les plus beaux paysages. Un système éducatif qui ne demande qu'à être remis au goût du jour. Et on a une intelligence énorme, notamment dans nos élites. Il faut juste qu'elles arrêtent d'être gauchistes. Mais ça va venir. | **L3 컴퓨팅/가속기** | **NVIDIA** (NVDA 🇺🇸) | 2026-08-28 | [𝕏 Radar] SemiAnalysis(SemiAnalysis_): Most production inference services run on Kubernetes, where declarative configuration helps coordinate distributed services. Running one inference server in a Slurm job is easy; running a production-style topology across multiple containers and nodes is not.  NVIDIA’s open-source srt-slurm (https://t.co/Q7BXRQwFeb) provides a YAML-based orchestration layer for these deployments. It coordinates sbatch and srun, GPU placement, networking, readiness checks, and cleanup for systems involving prefill/decode disaggregation, multiple workers, Dynamo frontends, KV-cache-aware routers, and KV-cache offloading services.  It’s useful for quickly deploying representative inference systems for testing or benchmarking on GPU clusters that already run Slurm.  Great work to @0xishand  and the rest of the @NVIDIAAI  team on this! | 상대: ASE | - | 💬 분석가전망 | `[C2]` |

| **L3 컴퓨팅/가속기** | **NVIDIA** (NVDA 🇺🇸) | 2026-09-09 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): I learned a lot from this podcast episode.  You should all watch it.  To share the insights I took away:  1. Even if you secure additional GPUs or memory, total system output cannot increase if other segments such as substrates, MLCCs, and wafer testing are in short supply. In other words, in the semiconductor industry, total shipments are determined by the single most constrained component or process.  2. Substrate, power and analog semiconductor, foundry, and memory suppliers have historically been conservative and have not made new greenfield investments until they were confident in long term customer demand. In response, Broadcom, Nvidia, and others signed sufficiently long LTAs with their supply chains and gave them visibility, and that point in time was precisely late 2025 to early 2026. Working backward from there to when a new production fab comes online, meaningful new capacity ramp and production will begin in 2028, not 2027. That means next year, 2027, could be the most supply constrained year in history.  3. In 2028, supply will still be short and the shortage will not be fully resolved, but it will be the year when the largest amount of new production capacity in the history of the semiconductor industry comes online at once. This is an opportunity for additional revenue.  4. The current supply constraint is a safety net that delays overinvestment. In past capital intensive infrastructure booms such as railroads, canals, 3G, and mobile phones, people always overbuilt. But the difference between past cycles and this AI infrastructure cycle is that even with demand in place, production facilities and data centers cannot be built as quickly as desired. So the current supply constraint can paradoxically be seen as playing a positive role in the sense that it limits excessive investment. The explanation is that by the time the supply chain catches up (2028), demand and industry conditions may have normalized to some degree as well. In enterprise AI too, high token costs and limited access to compute are slowing the pace of deployment, and if costs were much lower, companies would have moved more experiments into production environments faster. | 상대: Broadcom, ASE | 제품: TPU | - | ⚠️ 공급망속보 |

| **L3 컴퓨팅/가속기** | **NVIDIA** (NVDA 🇺🇸) | 2026-09-18 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): SK hynix Subsidiary Solidigm Pursues Its First U.S. NAND Fab  Solidigm, the NAND subsidiary of SK hynix, is reviewing the construction of its first NAND mass production line in the United States, it was learned on the 19th. The company is currently working through details such as the fab site and the timing of the investment.  With NAND demand surging on the expansion of AI infrastructure buildouts, the move is being read as a strategy to strengthen cooperation with customers in the U.S. It also lines up with the push to expand U.S. AI investment that SK Chairman Chey Tae won has been leading at the group level.  Solidigm is the U.S. entity SK hynix established when it acquired Intel's NAND flash business at the end of 2021. The total deal size was $9 billion (about 10 trillion won at the exchange rate of the time). Solidigm operates Fab 1 in Dalian, China, and began capital spending on Dalian Fab 2 in the third quarter of this year. In the U.S. it has only its headquarters and an R&D center, with no production base.  The region Solidigm is considering for its first U.S. production base is the East Coast. The company is understood to be discussing the details, including site selection, product mix and the timing of mass production.  A person familiar with the matter said, "Solidigm is pursuing a local mass production line to respond to NAND demand for AI servers from U.S. big tech companies," adding that "it will firm up its investment plan based on long term agreement (LTA) negotiations with customers."  Solidigm has so far focused on developing quad level cell (QLC) NAND for AI data centers. QLC stores four bits in a single cell, the smallest unit of data storage. Compared with other approaches such as triple level cell (TLC), which stores three bits, it is better suited to building high capacity SSDs.  Orders for high capacity NAND have been piling up in particular as the NAND capacity required by next generation AI platforms such as Nvidia's Blackwell and Vera Rubin climbs sharply. According to market research firm TrendForce, NAND revenue at SK hynix (including Solidigm) reached $14.2729 billion (about 19.8 trillion won) in the second quarter of this year, up 89.5% from the previous quarter.  Solidigm's U.S. investment fits with the broader expansion of U.S. investment that SK Group is pursuing. Following the listing of SK hynix American depositary receipts (ADR) and the construction of an advanced packaging fab in the U.S., SK Group is also leaving the door open to building an additional memory production plant. Solidigm itself is preparing an initial public offering (IPO) on the Nasdaq.  At an event in New York in July marking the SK hynix ADR listing, Chairman Chey said, "In addition to the advanced packaging facility in Indiana, we are reviewing further investments," and "if conditions such as power, water, workforce and supply chain are in place, building a memory production plant is also possible."  More recently, talk of SK hynix and Intel cooperating on memory production in the U.S. has also surfaced through foreign media and industry sources. It will take time before anything is finalized, but the industry view is that it is feasible, given that U.S. demand for AI memory such as high bandwidth memory (HBM) is expected to keep growing over the medium to long term.  Regarding its U.S. capital investment plans, a Solidigm official said, "We are reviewing various options to strengthen our business competitiveness, but nothing has been decided."  $SKHY | 상대: Intel, ASE | 제품: Blackwell B200, Rubin R100 | 금액: $9 | 💬 분석가전망 |

| **L3 컴퓨팅/가속기** | **NVIDIA** (NVDA 🇺🇸) | 2026-09-20 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Samsung Electronics to Double HBM4 and HBM4E Production Next Year  Samsung Electronics is expected to at least double its production of the HBM4 family next year compared with this year, including sixth generation high bandwidth memory (HBM4) and seventh generation HBM (HBM4E). This follows its plan to raise demand for "glass carriers," the glass supports that hold wafers while DRAM for HBM is thinned, to 2.5 times this year's level.  According to the semiconductor industry on the 20th, Samsung Electronics will increase its outsourced cleaning volume of glass carriers, which are essential to the HBM production process, from 20,000 units per month this year to 50,000 units per month next year. Only 10,000 glass carriers per month were needed last year, which means the volume doubles this year versus last year and rises 2.5 times next year versus this year.  A glass carrier is a support temporarily attached to the underside of a DRAM wafer for HBM to keep the wafer from warping or cracking while it is ground thin and holes are drilled through it. Because HBM must stack multiple DRAM dies within a limited thickness, wafer thinning technology and warpage control processes become more important as the stack count rises.  HBM4 and HBM4E, for which Samsung Electronics is especially preparing to expand production, center on high stack products of 12-High and above. The industry's analysis is that, even after accounting for the fact that glass carriers are reused after cleaning and that usage varies with how they are deployed at each process step and with yield, a 2.5 times increase in the related volume makes it highly likely that HBM4 and HBM4E production will be at least double this year's level.  In February this year, Samsung Electronics began mass production shipments of HBM4, which applies sixth generation 10nm class (1c) DRAM and a base die built on a 4 nanometer (nm, one billionth of a meter) process. In May, it also provided HBM4E 12-High samples to customers including Nvidia.  The industry expects Samsung Electronics' HBM production scale, measured by monthly average wafer input, to grow nearly 40% from about 180,000 wafers this year to about 250,000 wafers next year. In terms of shipment mix by product, some also forecast that the HBM4 family will rise from around 40% this year to about 80% next year as HBM4E mass production gets into full swing. An industry official explained, "As Samsung ramps up HBM production, it appears to be placing HBM4, a high value added product, at the center." | 상대: ASE, Samsung | 제품: HBM4, HBM | - | 💬 분석가전망 |

Goldman Sachs expects Samsung’s HBM revenue to explode from roughly $20 billion in 2026 to $74 billion in 2027 and that is 274% growth in a single year, with HBM rising from only 8% to 19% of Samsung’s total DRAM revenue.The jump is being driven by both volume and pricing. Goldman expects Samsung’s HBM shipments to grow more than 60%, while average selling prices rise roughly 130% as the product mix shifts toward HBM4. Samsung is not just selling more memory but rathermoving toward significantly more valuable memory. KB Securities expects HBM4 to represent around 80% of Samsung’s HBM revenue in 2027, up from roughly 40% in 2026.  Samsung began mass producing HBM4 in February, with shipments for Nvidia’s Vera Rubin platform expanding during the year. The company expects its third quarter HBM4 revenue to more than triple sequentially, while HBM4 should represent over 60% of second-half HBM revenue. This also strengthens the rest of Samsung’s memory business. HBM consumes more wafer capacity than conventional DRAM, so every production line shifted toward HBM removes supply from server and consumer memory. Samsung expects HBM to consume more than one-third of global DRAM wafer capacity in 2027. That means Samsung can benefit twice: once from selling enormous volumes of premium HBM at higher prices, and again from tighter supply supporting pricing across conventional DRAM.  The chart then shows HBM revenue moving above $100 billion in 2028, with its share of Samsung’s DRAM business continuing to rise. If that forecast is close, Samsung’s HBM operation alone could become larger than many entire semiconductor companies. The old bear case was that Samsung had fallen too far behind SK Hynix in HBM. But Samsung’s HBM revenue share already increased from 21% in Q1 2026 to 33% in Q2, while SK Hynix fell from 58% to 50%.  Samsung is no longer simply catching up but rather ramping the highest value memory product in the world while its scale in conventional DRAM, NAND, foundry and advanced packaging gives it multiple ways to benefit from the same AI buildout.That is why the Samsung story is changing. The market still remembers the company that missed the early HBM cycle, while the numbers increasingly point toward a company that could become one of its largest winners. | 상대: ASE, SK hynix | 제품: Rubin R100, HBM4 | 금액: $20 | 💬 분석가전망 |

| **L4 파운드리/제조** | **ASML** (ASML 🇳🇱) | 2026-10-02 | [𝕏 Radar] SemiconductorsX(@SemiconductorsX): The plate under the AI chip is the new heat problem. Samsung is treating HBM4E as the point where that has to be solved.  Today’s AI interposer is already about 5.5 times the biggest single EUV shot, 858 mm². An equipment source says the plate HBM sits on could go 9x, then 12x, then 40x.  The interposer is the board that ties the GPU and the HBM stacks together. A bigger plate holds more compute dies and more memory. It is growing because one advanced chip cannot carry the AI load.   Chiplets on a 2.5D package are now the server standard. On-chip SRAM also barely shrinks, so the memory next to the compute die matters more.  It sees seventh-generation HBM4E, due for real volume next year, as the inflection. It shipped the industry’s first HBM4E 12-high samples in May.   The stack is said to use 1c DRAM and a 4 nm base die. SK Hynix sent 12-high HBM4E samples to major customers in June.  Heat is the constraint. Low-power design is the base. Hybrid copper bonding drops the bumps between DRAM layers, thins the stack, and lets heat out. It showed that at GTC in March.   Heat path block is the fallback that cuts heat without hybrid bonding. It is also looking at package and system cooling with tool and backend partners. | 상대: ASE, SK hynix | 제품: HBM4, HBM | - | 💬 분석가전망 |

New tools are next.  TheElec, October 10, exclusive: the hike covers all EUV and DUV replacement parts, from January. Headquarters sent the proposal through the Korean unit in early September. Purchasing teams negotiated, then signed.  It covers routine consumables and the big pieces that get swapped when something fails or drifts. Lenses, mirrors, light sources, precision drives. The cost of keeping a scanner running just went up.  It used to reprice single parts when a material spiked. A blanket 10% is unusual. One industry source told the paper costs are up across the bill, and it is also taking more margin because the customers are making money.  Service, parts and upgrades are a separate line. Last year that was €8.193 billion, 25% of €32.667 billion. It has guided that line up more than 30% this year. It is also in talks with major customers on higher prices for new tools.  The Information reported in July that it notified Chinese buyers of a 10% DUV tool increase, and some accepted. TSMC, the largest customer, is said to be pushing back.  The industry read is that Samsung and SK Hynix accepting a blanket hike gives cover to everyone else. Applied Materials said in August that prices are higher on new and existing tools. Tokyo Electron said in late July it is adjusting prices, including tools already on sale, against higher costs. Advanced Energy has raised some product lines.  The backdrop is the same shortage. AI data-center spend has memory and foundry expanding at once. Tool and parts orders piled up. Adding capacity takes time. Materials and inflation raised the supplier’s cost. The buyer used to demand a cut. The supplier now sets the price.  One source added the next step. More wafer starts will pull materials into the same shortage.  If the spare-parts line is already a quarter of sales and rising more than 30%, is the 10% a cost pass-through or the installed base becoming the margin? | 상대: TSMC, Applied Materials | 제품: EUV/DUV | 금액: €8.193 | ⚠️ 공급망속보 |

| **L4 파운드리/제조** | **Applied Materials** (AMAT 🇺🇸) | 2026-09-09 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Micron, Semiconductor Substrate Makers Show Interest in JDI's Mobara Plant Sale  Japanese display maker Japan Display Inc. (JDI) has drawn interest in the sale of its Mobara plant (fab), which halted operations this March, not only from Micron of the United States and several data center operators but also from a semiconductor substrate maker, according to sources on the 8th.  The JDI Mobara plant previously produced sixth generation liquid crystal displays (LCD) and organic light emitting diodes (OLED). It also mass produced OLED panels for the Apple Watch until the second half of 2025. As JDI had announced in the first quarter of 2025, it halted operations at Mobara in March 2026.  Industry source A said, "Micron and several data center operators have submitted letters of intent to JDI to acquire the Mobara plant." Another source B said, "Micron and a Korean semiconductor substrate maker, among others, are in talks with JDI over purchasing the Mobara plant." At its quarterly earnings announcement in early August, JDI stated that it was in discussions with multiple companies regarding the sale of the plant.  The reason Micron, data center operators, and semiconductor substrate makers are interested in the Mobara plant comes down to time. Building a new plant from scratch and securing government permits would take years before mass production could begin. Buying the Mobara plant shortens that timeline accordingly.  Because Mobara previously produced sixth generation LCD and OLED panels, its cleanroom cleanliness levels make it unsuitable for semiconductor front end processing, but the site can be used as a data center or as a back end packaging plant for products such as glass substrates. That is why Micron, data center operators, and substrate makers are interested. The sale price for the plant is currently reported to be under discussion at around KRW 500 billion, though the figure could change depending on circumstances.  OLEDWorks, a US OLED company working with JDI, is reported to be interested not in the Mobara plant but in the 4.5 generation Ishikawa plant. Ishikawa has a line capable of backplane (BP) processing. For OLEDWorks, securing a plant that can reliably handle BP processing going forward is a necessity.  JDI is currently considering selling its plants, including Mobara and Ishikawa, separately to different buyers. If each of these deals goes through, the ownership of JDI's plants would change hands individually.  JDI was formed in 2012 under the lead of the Japanese government (Innovation Network Corporation of Japan) by consolidating the LCD businesses of Sony, Toshiba, and Hitachi. It subsequently failed to respond to market shifts such as the growing adoption of OLED in smartphones and fell behind Korean and Chinese panel makers in competition. In November 2024, JDI unveiled a new catchphrase, "Beyond Display." Whereas the old JDI consisted solely of displays, the company said the new JDI would comprise displays, sensors, artificial intelligence (AI) data centers, and advanced semiconductor packaging.  Meanwhile, most of the equipment related to eLEAP, the technology JDI had developed at the Mobara plant, was purchased by China's HKC. eLEAP patterns red, green, and blue (RGB) OLED subpixels through a photolithography process without using a fine metal mask (FMM). Its viability for mass production has yet to be verified. The technology is similar in principle to FLiPP, which LG Display unveiled at IMID this August.  The appeal of JDI's eLEAP patents is limited. This is because JDI is reported to have sold licenses (rights of use) for the patents to Applied Materials (AMAT), which makes the deposition equipment required to implement eLEAP, and others. JDI sells the licenses to AMAT and others and collects royalties, which means a third party that buys the patents from JDI cannot use them exclusively. Several panel makers in Korea and abroad are developing eLEAP type technology, but there is little incentive to purchase the JDI patents.  $MU | 상대: Meta, Intel | - | ⚠️ 공급망속보 | `[C2]` |

That date could be beaten if iPhones with A20 / A20 Pro chips inside – also TSMC 2nm – ship earlier. The iPhone event is widely expected Sept. 9, with shipping typically a week later (Sept. 18?).  By “first to market,” I mean a finished product actually available to customers — not announced, sampling or in production.  Apple has been first to market with every major new TSMC process generation for nearly a decade: 10nm, 7nm, 5nm and 3nm.  The streak goes back at least to the 10nm A10X in the iPad Pro in June 2017.  AMD reached an important 2nm milestone first: In May it called Venice “the first high-performance computing (HPC) product in the industry to enter production on TSMC’s advanced 2nm process technology.”  That’s true. Even TSMC underscored the impact of T-glass on CoWoS yield and reliability. We see Chinese vendors are entering aggressively in the T-glass market, expanding supply from a single source (Nittobo) to more than five suppliers, offering a glimmer of hope on the T-glass supply side. Materials are tight beyond just T-glass; substrate-level CCL has been almost solely sourced from Resonac, creating another bottleneck for substrates. Now, we are also seeing EMC make continued progress on substrate CCL. Product shipments could begin as early as 4Q26, with plans to expand its substrate CCL capacity by 100% in 2027, making a new solution available for the substrate CCL. | **L4 파운드리/제조** | **TSMC** (TSM 🇹🇼) | 2026-09-14 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Semiconductor Supply Chain News Roundup, September 14  1. Samsung Electro-Mechanics submitted TGV module samples for its glass substrate to TSMC for reliability verification two to three months ago, but the evaluation schedule has reportedly been delayed by more than a month.  As a result, the ordering schedule for some TGV related equipment that Samsung Electro-Mechanics had been preparing with its suppliers is understood to have been pushed back from the original plan. Samsung Electro-Mechanics had originally targeted glass substrate mass production around 2027, building a pilot line and running customer evaluations. Earlier this year it revised the target for full operation to 2028, but with reliability verification and mass production process stabilization taking longer than expected, equipment investment is also not proceeding as originally planned.  2. A senior executive at Samsung Electro-Mechanics recently conveyed to employees a policy of moderating the pace of investment in the glass substrate business for the time being.  Rather than concentrating large scale investment on glass substrates, the direction is to prioritize investment in existing package substrate businesses such as FC-BGA and in high value added businesses with confirmed demand, such as MLCCs for AI and automotive applications. Looking at the current state of Samsung Electro-Mechanics' glass substrate work, yields in the initial process of forming holes in the glass through laser processing and etching have risen to 90% or above, but in the subsequent processes of metallizing and filling the holes with copper, yields reportedly drop to around 30%. After that, additional steps are still required, including chemical mechanical polishing (CMP), which flattens the substrate surface using chemical reactions and mechanical force, as well as cutting, and by that point yields become far too low. That said, some argue that this adjustment itself should not be read as a delay in the glass substrate business as such. The market has not yet opened in earnest, and the actual customers, global semiconductor companies, are still carrying out their own preparation and validation for glass substrate adoption.  3. SK hynix is moving to lock in its equipment supply chain. The approach is to share medium to long term demand forecasts earlier than before so that suppliers can secure in advance the equipment and key components needed for future investment.  Previously, SK hynix shared investment plans with equipment makers covering about six months, or at most about a year. Going forward, it plans to present demand not only for next year but also for the year after, allowing suppliers to plan their production capacity and component procurement ahead of time.  4. SEMES, Samsung Electronics' semiconductor equipment subsidiary, is firming up the component supply chain for the hybrid bonder to be applied to next generation high bandwidth memory (HBM). Specifically, SEMES has signed a supply agreement with the Korean subsidiary of HIWIN, a Taiwanese motion control company, for the Single Axis Stage, a precision drive component to be used in the hybrid bonder, and is currently conducting tests. HIWIN's product is being validated against SEMES' hybrid bonder specifications, and testing is understood to be in its final stages. The single axis stage is a drive component that supports precise position control in the hybrid bonder. Because a hybrid bonder must align and bond chips at extremely fine pitches, it requires a high level of positional accuracy. SEMES is said to be conducting equipment testing after signing a supply contract with a customer for a D2W (Die to Wafer) hybrid bonder for HBM. The industry views that customer as its parent company, Samsung Electronics.  5. PCB orders for SOCAMM2 destined for major memory makers including Samsung Electronics and SK hynix are growing beyond the levels planned at the start of the year.  The increase in orders for DDR5 RDIMM PCBs is especially pronounced. Compared with the order plans projected at the start of the year, the upward revision for RDIMM is understood to be even larger than that for SOCAMM2. An industry source cited by the press said, "For SOCAMM2, order volumes are growing beyond what we expected at the start of the year, and we expect them to increase further into the second half," adding, "RDIMM orders are rising even more steeply relative to the beginning of year plan than SOCAMM2." The source also noted, "Some customers have already begun discussing advance orders for next year's volumes." | 상대: Meta, ASE | 제품: HBM | - | ⚠️ 공급망속보 |

| **L4 파운드리/제조** | **TSMC** (TSM 🇹🇼) | 2026-09-15 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): According to Korean media reports, Samsung Foundry's Taylor fab has begun operating earlier than the originally planned November start and has recently reached a utilization rate of 30%.  Utilization crossed 20% as of last month and has approached 30% this month.  Samsung Electronics held an equipment move in ceremony at the site in April and completed a tape out in July, the step that hands a chip design over to actual wafer fabrication.  A tape out is normally followed by prototype production and verification, but Samsung is said to have pulled its mass production schedule forward to keep up with surging AI chip orders. With the foundry lines at its Hwaseong and Pyeongtaek campuses in Korea, which handle the leading edge nodes (2 to 4nm), running at full capacity, the company accelerated the production schedule at Taylor.  The customer that triggered the early ramp at Taylor is Tesla. The key product to be made there is Tesla's next generation AI5 chip. Tesla, which is accelerating its investments in full self driving, robotics, and AI data centers, is understood to be demanding a rapid supply expansion of AI5, which it plans to use across the Model 3 and Model Y, the Cybercab robotaxi, the Optimus humanoid robot, and its AI data centers.  With Taylor utilization, currently around 30%, climbing quickly, some in the industry expect it to reach 100%, effectively full utilization, by around year end.  Alongside raising utilization at Taylor, Samsung has also begun preparations to build a second Taylor fab, targeting groundbreaking by year end. The industry speculates that the second fab, expected to come online around 2030, could introduce Samsung's next generation 1.4nm process.  The expansion at Taylor also ties into Samsung's broader strategy to strengthen its foundry business. Taiwan's TSMC, the industry leader, has seen most of its advanced node capacity absorbed by a flood of AI chip orders. Samsung plans to take advantage of this, using the early ramp at Taylor as a springboard to absorb Tesla's volume reliably while pursuing additional customers. If Taylor utilization rises quickly, the foundry division's return to profitability is also expected to come sooner. | 상대: ASE, Samsung | 시설: 테일러 팹 | - | 💬 분석가전망 |

(YMTC), China's largest NAND flash maker, has started connecting production equipment at its third fab in Wuhan (FAB3). With preparations for bringing the fab online entering the final stage, initial operation by the end of the year is coming into view.  According to Chinese procurement networks on the 14th, China Electronics System Engineering No. 2 Construction Co. (CESE No. 2), a state owned contractor under China Electronics Corporation (CEC), recently began selecting a construction firm for "Gas Section 3" of the "Wuhan YMTC FAB3 Secondary Piping Zone A, Package B Process Equipment Piping General Contracting Project."  The work is secondary piping, which connects the utility supply network already built into the fab, including gas and water, to individual pieces of production equipment. Gas piping delivers the specialty gases used in semiconductor manufacturing steps such as etching and deposition to the production tools.  This is read as YMTC expanding the work needed to bring already installed production equipment into operation. Bidders were required to have a track record on projects affiliated with YMTC or Chinese DRAM maker CXMT, or on a 12 inch semiconductor fab of equivalent scale.  YMTC's Wuhan Fab 3 is reportedly set to begin initial operation at the end of this year. At full capacity, output is understood to be around 100,000 wafers per month on a 12 inch basis. After initial production, capacity is expected to be expanded in stages through further equipment move in and yield stabilization.  The Fab 3 expansion draws attention because it coincides with YMTC's rapid market share gains. According to Counterpoint Research, YMTC accounted for 14% of global NAND bit shipments in the second quarter of this year, overtaking Kioxia to become the world's No. 3. Samsung Electronics ranked first at 25% and SK hynix second at 22%.  Once YMTC brings its new Wuhan capacity fully online, supply competition in the NAND market is expected to intensify further. In particular, since YMTC has been securing new capacity by raising the share of Chinese made equipment despite US export controls on advanced semiconductor equipment, attention is on whether it will close the gap with incumbent NAND makers such as Samsung Electronics and SK hynix at a faster pace.  An industry source said, "YMTC's capacity expansion is unlikely to lead to a NAND oversupply right away," but added, "Because it is growing output and share on the back of China's domestic market, it could become a medium to long term competitive burden for the existing NAND makers." | 상대: Amazon, ASE | 제품: TPU | - | ⚠️ 공급망속보 |

| **L5 메모리/스토리지** | **Micron** (MU 🇺🇸) | 2026-09-09 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek push to commercialize substrates for Intel's 'EMIB-T', challenging Japan's Ibiden  Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek are pursuing the supply of high value added semiconductor substrates for Intel's Embedded Multi-die Interconnect Bridge (EMIB). EMIB is an advanced packaging technology whose demand has recently been growing in the high performance artificial intelligence (AI) semiconductor market. Japan's Ibiden is the leading company in this field.  According to industry sources on the 9th, Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek are developing and testing products with the goal of entering the package substrate supply chain for Intel's EMIB-T.  Intel's EMIB-T gains traction in the AI chip market, drawing interest from Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek  EMIB is an advanced packaging method that has recently been drawing attention in the AI semiconductor industry. Currently, the industry inserts a thin layer called a silicon interposer to connect high bandwidth memory (HBM) and system semiconductors into a single package. Using an interposer allows the circuits of each chip to be connected more densely. This is commonly referred to as '2.5D packaging'.  EMIB connects chips to one another using small silicon bridges instead of an interposer. It is a technology that places silicon bridges only where they are needed. It is more cost efficient than an interposer. Another advantage is that chips can be placed with more flexibility.  Because Intel had previously used EMIB only for manufacturing its own chips, market demand was limited. However, with Intel recently developing an improved version called 'EMIB-T', external sales are now expanding in earnest. Google will also apply EMIB-T for the first time in its next generation AI accelerator (TPU), scheduled for release in the second half of next year.  EMIB-T is a technology that inserts through-silicon vias (TSVs), which serve as power delivery pathways, into the silicon bridge. By shortening the power delivery path between the substrate and the chip through TSVs, it improves power efficiency and signal integrity.  Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek have also taken note of the growth of EMIB-T and are attempting to enter the supply chain for its dedicated package substrates. The product embeds a silicon bridge into a flip chip ball grid array (FC-BGA), the existing package substrate for high performance semiconductors, making it technically demanding.  In the case of Samsung Electro-Mechanics, the company is developing the substrate under the concept of a 2.1D package substrate. A person familiar with the matter said, "Samsung Electro-Mechanics has been developing substrate samples for EMIB for several years, and following the recent trend toward EMIB-T commercialization, it is actively pushing to enter the supply chain."  LG Innotek recently sent package substrates for EMIB-T to SK hynix, which is conducting sample tests. LG Innotek and SK hynix are reportedly mounting HBM on the substrate and evaluating the product's characteristics.  Challenging FC-BGA leader Ibiden of Japan, but large scale investment is a burden  Entering the package substrate supply chain for Intel's EMIB-T is one of the key tasks for Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek in expanding their high value added FC-BGA businesses, which is their goal.  Currently, package substrates for EMIB-T are supplied by four companies: Ibiden and Shinko Electric of Japan, Unimicron of Taiwan, and AT&S of Austria.  Ibiden is the number one company in the FC-BGA industry. It has built a solid cooperative relationship with Intel. In the first half of this year, it decided to begin construction of a mass production line dedicated to substrates for Intel's EMIB-T, utilizing an existing idle fab. The investment is approximately 2 trillion won. It is reported to have received advance payments from Big Tech companies including Google, Amazon, and Intel.  As latecomers to the relevant supply chain, Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek are expected to have to overcome technological and market challenges in order to commercialize substrates for EMIB-T.  An industry official in the substrate sector explained, "Ibiden, Unimicron, and others have built stable technological capabilities and production capacity by mass producing substrates for Intel's EMIB for seven to eight years," adding, "It is true that business opportunities are expanding with EMIB-T, but domestic companies face the risk of having to make large scale investments first."  $INTC | 상대: Amazon, Google | 제품: TPU, HBM | - | 💬 분석가전망 |
| **L5 메모리/스토리지** | **Micron** (MU 🇺🇸) | 2026-09-09 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Micron, Semiconductor Substrate Makers Show Interest in JDI's Mobara Plant Sale  Japanese display maker Japan Display Inc. (JDI) has drawn interest in the sale of its Mobara plant (fab), which halted operations this March, not only from Micron of the United States and several data center operators but also from a semiconductor substrate maker, according to sources on the 8th.  The JDI Mobara plant previously produced sixth generation liquid crystal displays (LCD) and organic light emitting diodes (OLED). It also mass produced OLED panels for the Apple Watch until the second half of 2025. As JDI had announced in the first quarter of 2025, it halted operations at Mobara in March 2026.  Industry source A said, "Micron and several data center operators have submitted letters of intent to JDI to acquire the Mobara plant." Another source B said, "Micron and a Korean semiconductor substrate maker, among others, are in talks with JDI over purchasing the Mobara plant." At its quarterly earnings announcement in early August, JDI stated that it was in discussions with multiple companies regarding the sale of the plant.  The reason Micron, data center operators, and semiconductor substrate makers are interested in the Mobara plant comes down to time. Building a new plant from scratch and securing government permits would take years before mass production could begin. Buying the Mobara plant shortens that timeline accordingly.  Because Mobara previously produced sixth generation LCD and OLED panels, its cleanroom cleanliness levels make it unsuitable for semiconductor front end processing, but the site can be used as a data center or as a back end packaging plant for products such as glass substrates. That is why Micron, data center operators, and substrate makers are interested. The sale price for the plant is currently reported to be under discussion at around KRW 500 billion, though the figure could change depending on circumstances.  OLEDWorks, a US OLED company working with JDI, is reported to be interested not in the Mobara plant but in the 4.5 generation Ishikawa plant. Ishikawa has a line capable of backplane (BP) processing. For OLEDWorks, securing a plant that can reliably handle BP processing going forward is a necessity.  JDI is currently considering selling its plants, including Mobara and Ishikawa, separately to different buyers. If each of these deals goes through, the ownership of JDI's plants would change hands individually.  JDI was formed in 2012 under the lead of the Japanese government (Innovation Network Corporation of Japan) by consolidating the LCD businesses of Sony, Toshiba, and Hitachi. It subsequently failed to respond to market shifts such as the growing adoption of OLED in smartphones and fell behind Korean and Chinese panel makers in competition. In November 2024, JDI unveiled a new catchphrase, "Beyond Display." Whereas the old JDI consisted solely of displays, the company said the new JDI would comprise displays, sensors, artificial intelligence (AI) data centers, and advanced semiconductor packaging.  Meanwhile, most of the equipment related to eLEAP, the technology JDI had developed at the Mobara plant, was purchased by China's HKC. eLEAP patterns red, green, and blue (RGB) OLED subpixels through a photolithography process without using a fine metal mask (FMM). Its viability for mass production has yet to be verified. The technology is similar in principle to FLiPP, which LG Display unveiled at IMID this August.  The appeal of JDI's eLEAP patents is limited. This is because JDI is reported to have sold licenses (rights of use) for the patents to Applied Materials (AMAT), which makes the deposition equipment required to implement eLEAP, and others. JDI sells the licenses to AMAT and others and collects royalties, which means a third party that buys the patents from JDI cannot use them exclusively. Several panel makers in Korea and abroad are developing eLEAP type technology, but there is little incentive to purchase the JDI patents.  $MU | 상대: Meta, Intel | - | ⚠️ 공급망속보 | `[C2]` |

| **L5 메모리/스토리지** | **SK hynix** (000660 🇰🇷) | 2026-09-09 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek push to commercialize substrates for Intel's 'EMIB-T', challenging Japan's Ibiden  Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek are pursuing the supply of high value added semiconductor substrates for Intel's Embedded Multi-die Interconnect Bridge (EMIB). EMIB is an advanced packaging technology whose demand has recently been growing in the high performance artificial intelligence (AI) semiconductor market. Japan's Ibiden is the leading company in this field.  According to industry sources on the 9th, Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek are developing and testing products with the goal of entering the package substrate supply chain for Intel's EMIB-T.  Intel's EMIB-T gains traction in the AI chip market, drawing interest from Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek  EMIB is an advanced packaging method that has recently been drawing attention in the AI semiconductor industry. Currently, the industry inserts a thin layer called a silicon interposer to connect high bandwidth memory (HBM) and system semiconductors into a single package. Using an interposer allows the circuits of each chip to be connected more densely. This is commonly referred to as '2.5D packaging'.  EMIB connects chips to one another using small silicon bridges instead of an interposer. It is a technology that places silicon bridges only where they are needed. It is more cost efficient than an interposer. Another advantage is that chips can be placed with more flexibility.  Because Intel had previously used EMIB only for manufacturing its own chips, market demand was limited. However, with Intel recently developing an improved version called 'EMIB-T', external sales are now expanding in earnest. Google will also apply EMIB-T for the first time in its next generation AI accelerator (TPU), scheduled for release in the second half of next year.  EMIB-T is a technology that inserts through-silicon vias (TSVs), which serve as power delivery pathways, into the silicon bridge. By shortening the power delivery path between the substrate and the chip through TSVs, it improves power efficiency and signal integrity.  Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek have also taken note of the growth of EMIB-T and are attempting to enter the supply chain for its dedicated package substrates. The product embeds a silicon bridge into a flip chip ball grid array (FC-BGA), the existing package substrate for high performance semiconductors, making it technically demanding.  In the case of Samsung Electro-Mechanics, the company is developing the substrate under the concept of a 2.1D package substrate. A person familiar with the matter said, "Samsung Electro-Mechanics has been developing substrate samples for EMIB for several years, and following the recent trend toward EMIB-T commercialization, it is actively pushing to enter the supply chain."  LG Innotek recently sent package substrates for EMIB-T to SK hynix, which is conducting sample tests. LG Innotek and SK hynix are reportedly mounting HBM on the substrate and evaluating the product's characteristics.  Challenging FC-BGA leader Ibiden of Japan, but large scale investment is a burden  Entering the package substrate supply chain for Intel's EMIB-T is one of the key tasks for Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek in expanding their high value added FC-BGA businesses, which is their goal.  Currently, package substrates for EMIB-T are supplied by four companies: Ibiden and Shinko Electric of Japan, Unimicron of Taiwan, and AT&S of Austria.  Ibiden is the number one company in the FC-BGA industry. It has built a solid cooperative relationship with Intel. In the first half of this year, it decided to begin construction of a mass production line dedicated to substrates for Intel's EMIB-T, utilizing an existing idle fab. The investment is approximately 2 trillion won. It is reported to have received advance payments from Big Tech companies including Google, Amazon, and Intel.  As latecomers to the relevant supply chain, Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek are expected to have to overcome technological and market challenges in order to commercialize substrates for EMIB-T.  An industry official in the substrate sector explained, "Ibiden, Unimicron, and others have built stable technological capabilities and production capacity by mass producing substrates for Intel's EMIB for seven to eight years," adding, "It is true that business opportunities are expanding with EMIB-T, but domestic companies face the risk of having to make large scale investments first."  $INTC | 상대: Amazon, Google | 제품: TPU, HBM | - | 💬 분석가전망 |

Rather than concentrating large scale investment on glass substrates, the direction is to prioritize investment in existing package substrate businesses such as FC-BGA and in high value added businesses with confirmed demand, such as MLCCs for AI and automotive applications. Looking at the current state of Samsung Electro-Mechanics' glass substrate work, yields in the initial process of forming holes in the glass through laser processing and etching have risen to 90% or above, but in the subsequent processes of metallizing and filling the holes with copper, yields reportedly drop to around 30%. After that, additional steps are still required, including chemical mechanical polishing (CMP), which flattens the substrate surface using chemical reactions and mechanical force, as well as cutting, and by that point yields become far too low. That said, some argue that this adjustment itself should not be read as a delay in the glass substrate business as such. The market has not yet opened in earnest, and the actual customers, global semiconductor companies, are still carrying out their own preparation and validation for glass substrate adoption.  3. The company is currently working through details such as the fab site and the timing of the investment.  With NAND demand surging on the expansion of AI infrastructure buildouts, the move is being read as a strategy to strengthen cooperation with customers in the U.S. It also lines up with the push to expand U.S. AI investment that SK Chairman Chey Tae won has been leading at the group level.  Solidigm is the U.S. entity SK hynix established when it acquired Intel's NAND flash business at the end of 2021. The total deal size was $9 billion (about 10 trillion won at the exchange rate of the time). Solidigm operates Fab 1 in Dalian, China, and began capital spending on Dalian Fab 2 in the third quarter of this year. In the U.S. it has only its headquarters and an R&D center, with no production base.  The region Solidigm is considering for its first U.S. production base is the East Coast. The company is understood to be discussing the details, including site selection, product mix and the timing of mass production.  A person familiar with the matter said, "Solidigm is pursuing a local mass production line to respond to NAND demand for AI servers from U.S. big tech companies," adding that "it will firm up its investment plan based on long term agreement (LTA) negotiations with customers."  Solidigm has so far focused on developing quad level cell (QLC) NAND for AI data centers. QLC stores four bits in a single cell, the smallest unit of data storage. Compared with other approaches such as triple level cell (TLC), which stores three bits, it is better suited to building high capacity SSDs.  Orders for high capacity NAND have been piling up in particular as the NAND capacity required by next generation AI platforms such as Nvidia's Blackwell and Vera Rubin climbs sharply. According to market research firm TrendForce, NAND revenue at SK hynix (including Solidigm) reached $14.2729 billion (about 19.8 trillion won) in the second quarter of this year, up 89.5% from the previous quarter.  Solidigm's U.S. investment fits with the broader expansion of U.S. investment that SK Group is pursuing. Following the listing of SK hynix American depositary receipts (ADR) and the construction of an advanced packaging fab in the U.S., SK Group is also leaving the door open to building an additional memory production plant. Solidigm itself is preparing an initial public offering (IPO) on the Nasdaq.  At an event in New York in July marking the SK hynix ADR listing, Chairman Chey said, "In addition to the advanced packaging facility in Indiana, we are reviewing further investments," and "if conditions such as power, water, workforce and supply chain are in place, building a memory production plant is also possible."  More recently, talk of SK hynix and Intel cooperating on memory production in the U.S. has also surfaced through foreign media and industry sources. It will take time before anything is finalized, but the industry view is that it is feasible, given that U.S. demand for AI memory such as high bandwidth memory (HBM) is expected to keep growing over the medium to long term.  Regarding its U.S. capital investment plans, a Solidigm official said, "We are reviewing various options to strengthen our business competitiveness, but nothing has been decided."  $SKHY | 상대: NVIDIA, Intel | 제품: Blackwell B200, Rubin R100 | 금액: $9 | 💬 분석가전망 |
