This follows its plan to raise demand for "glass carriers," the glass supports that hold wafers while DRAM for HBM is thinned, to 2.5 times this year's level.  According to the semiconductor industry on the 20th, Samsung Electronics will increase its outsourced cleaning volume of glass carriers, which are essential to the HBM production process, from 20,000 units per month this year to 50,000 units per month next year. Only 10,000 glass carriers per month were needed last year, which means the volume doubles this year versus last year and rises 2.5 times next year versus this year.  A glass carrier is a support temporarily attached to the underside of a DRAM wafer for HBM to keep the wafer from warping or cracking while it is ground thin and holes are drilled through it. Because HBM must stack multiple DRAM dies within a limited thickness, wafer thinning technology and warpage control processes become more important as the stack count rises.  HBM4 and HBM4E, for which Samsung Electronics is especially preparing to expand production, center on high stack products of 12-High and above. The industry's analysis is that, even after accounting for the fact that glass carriers are reused after cleaning and that usage varies with how they are deployed at each process step and with yield, a 2.5 times increase in the related volume makes it highly likely that HBM4 and HBM4E production will be at least double this year's level.  In February this year, Samsung Electronics began mass production shipments of HBM4, which applies sixth generation 10nm class (1c) DRAM and a base die built on a 4 nanometer (nm, one billionth of a meter) process. In May, it also provided HBM4E 12-High samples to customers including Nvidia.  The industry expects Samsung Electronics' HBM production scale, measured by monthly average wafer input, to grow nearly 40% from about 180,000 wafers this year to about 250,000 wafers next year. In terms of shipment mix by product, some also forecast that the HBM4 family will rise from around 40% this year to about 80% next year as HBM4E mass production gets into full swing. An industry official explained, "As Samsung ramps up HBM production, it appears to be placing HBM4, a high value added product, at the center." | 상대: NVIDIA, ASE | 제품: HBM4, HBM | - | 💬 분석가전망 |

| **L5 메모리/스토리지** | **삼성전자** (005930 🇰🇷) | 2026-09-09 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek push to commercialize substrates for Intel's 'EMIB-T', challenging Japan's Ibiden  Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek are pursuing the supply of high value added semiconductor substrates for Intel's Embedded Multi-die Interconnect Bridge (EMIB). EMIB is an advanced packaging technology whose demand has recently been growing in the high performance artificial intelligence (AI) semiconductor market. Japan's Ibiden is the leading company in this field.  According to industry sources on the 9th, Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek are developing and testing products with the goal of entering the package substrate supply chain for Intel's EMIB-T.  Intel's EMIB-T gains traction in the AI chip market, drawing interest from Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek  EMIB is an advanced packaging method that has recently been drawing attention in the AI semiconductor industry. Currently, the industry inserts a thin layer called a silicon interposer to connect high bandwidth memory (HBM) and system semiconductors into a single package. Using an interposer allows the circuits of each chip to be connected more densely. This is commonly referred to as '2.5D packaging'.  EMIB connects chips to one another using small silicon bridges instead of an interposer. It is a technology that places silicon bridges only where they are needed. It is more cost efficient than an interposer. Another advantage is that chips can be placed with more flexibility.  Because Intel had previously used EMIB only for manufacturing its own chips, market demand was limited. However, with Intel recently developing an improved version called 'EMIB-T', external sales are now expanding in earnest. Google will also apply EMIB-T for the first time in its next generation AI accelerator (TPU), scheduled for release in the second half of next year.  EMIB-T is a technology that inserts through-silicon vias (TSVs), which serve as power delivery pathways, into the silicon bridge. By shortening the power delivery path between the substrate and the chip through TSVs, it improves power efficiency and signal integrity.  Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek have also taken note of the growth of EMIB-T and are attempting to enter the supply chain for its dedicated package substrates. The product embeds a silicon bridge into a flip chip ball grid array (FC-BGA), the existing package substrate for high performance semiconductors, making it technically demanding.  In the case of Samsung Electro-Mechanics, the company is developing the substrate under the concept of a 2.1D package substrate. A person familiar with the matter said, "Samsung Electro-Mechanics has been developing substrate samples for EMIB for several years, and following the recent trend toward EMIB-T commercialization, it is actively pushing to enter the supply chain."  LG Innotek recently sent package substrates for EMIB-T to SK hynix, which is conducting sample tests. LG Innotek and SK hynix are reportedly mounting HBM on the substrate and evaluating the product's characteristics.  Challenging FC-BGA leader Ibiden of Japan, but large scale investment is a burden  Entering the package substrate supply chain for Intel's EMIB-T is one of the key tasks for Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek in expanding their high value added FC-BGA businesses, which is their goal.  Currently, package substrates for EMIB-T are supplied by four companies: Ibiden and Shinko Electric of Japan, Unimicron of Taiwan, and AT&S of Austria.  Ibiden is the number one company in the FC-BGA industry. It has built a solid cooperative relationship with Intel. In the first half of this year, it decided to begin construction of a mass production line dedicated to substrates for Intel's EMIB-T, utilizing an existing idle fab. The investment is approximately 2 trillion won. It is reported to have received advance payments from Big Tech companies including Google, Amazon, and Intel.  As latecomers to the relevant supply chain, Samsung Electro-Mechanics and LG Innotek are expected to have to overcome technological and market challenges in order to commercialize substrates for EMIB-T.  An industry official in the substrate sector explained, "Ibiden, Unimicron, and others have built stable technological capabilities and production capacity by mass producing substrates for Intel's EMIB for seven to eight years," adding, "It is true that business opportunities are expanding with EMIB-T, but domestic companies face the risk of having to make large scale investments first."  $INTC | 상대: Amazon, Google | 제품: TPU, HBM | - | 💬 분석가전망 |

| **L5 메모리/스토리지** | **삼성전자** (005930 🇰🇷) | 2026-09-10 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Taiwan's Shin Zu Shing supplies only "golden samples" of foldable iPhone hinges to Apple as production remains unstable  Production of the hinge for the iPhone Duo, Apple's first foldable phone unveiled on September 9 (local time), was found on September 10 to remain unstable. The hinge was also the component with the greatest impact on delays to the iPhone Duo production schedule.  The original first vendor for the iPhone Duo hinge was Amphenol of the United States, but Shin Zu Shing, which had been the second vendor, is now producing the hinge as the first vendor. This is because Amphenol's production yield fell far short of expectations.  Industry source A said, "Shin Zu Shing's hinge production yield is still low, and at present only 'golden samples' are being selected and sent to Foxconn." This means that among the hinges Shin Zu Shing makes, only those that meet Apple's standards are being picked out one by one and delivered. Another source, B, said, "Some components are experiencing production disruptions, but the Shin Zu Shing hinge is particularly unstable."  Apple designated multiple vendors for most of the remaining components, excluding items such as the organic light emitting diode (OLED) panel made by Samsung Display. Among iPhone Duo components, the hinge is reportedly the only one for which the first vendor is currently experiencing production disruptions.  Apple applied a "3D printed" hinge module to the iPhone Duo. A hinge module is generally made by combining multiple constituent parts, including gears, shafts, and mechanisms for free stop functionality. A 3D printed hinge module can improve the precision of these constituent parts.  When Apple unveiled the iPhone Duo on September 9, it stated that the hinge contains more than 100 constituent parts. Industry source C said, "Making a foldable phone thinner increases the number of hinge parts," adding, "The hinges in thin foldable phones released by Chinese smartphone makers had 120 to 130 parts." He said, "However, the count differs depending on whether hinge parts are limited to those directly tied to the drive mechanism or whether peripheral parts such as bolts and nuts are included," and added, "We will only know precisely after tearing down the iPhone Duo once it launches."  The change of first vendor is also presumed to be a factor behind Apple's difficulties with hinge production. Typically, the first vendor develops the component together with the set maker and finalizes the specifications. The second vendor is at a disadvantage because it must make the component to specifications that the first vendor has already finalized with the set maker. In effect, Shin Zu Shing is producing hinges to specifications that Amphenol previously finalized with Apple.  Supply of the backplate, another key iPhone Duo component, is also tight. The backplate is the part that supports the foldable panel when it is folded or unfolded. Fine holes are formed in the folding region to secure flexibility and durability. The second vendor for the backplate, China's Lingyi iTech, is reportedly struggling with mass production.  Although production of some iPhone Duo components is still not running smoothly, Apple has reportedly provided suppliers with a component production forecast sufficient to build around 5 million to 6 million finished iPhone Duo units by the end of this year. The number of components exceeds the number of finished units because Apple accounts for yield during final assembly as well as replacement demand from damage after the product is sold.  Apple unveiled the iPhone Duo on September 9, but preorders begin a month later on October 16. The launch date is October 23.  iPhone Duo pricing by storage capacity is $1,999 for 256GB (approximately 2.68 million won at current exchange rates), $2,199 for 512GB (approximately 2.95 million won), $2,599 for 1TB (approximately 3.49 million won), and $3,199 for 2TB (approximately 4.29 million won). Korean launch prices are 3.29 million won for 256GB, 3.59 million won for 512GB, 4.19 million won for 1TB, and 5.09 million won for 2TB.  Source B, quoted above, said, "No iPhone Duo buyer will choose the 256GB model," adding, "The 512GB model is the real 'base model,' and the product price should be viewed as 3 million won or more." By this view, the price of the "base model" (512GB) in the Korean market is 3.6 million won. | 상대: ASE, Apple | 금액: $1 | ⚠️ 공급망속보 | `[C2]` |

| **L5 메모리/스토리지** | **삼성전자** (005930 🇰🇷) | 2026-09-14 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Samsung Electro-Mechanics and Qualcomm Form 2.1D Packaging Alliance, Jointly Developing an "Organic Bridge" That Bypasses Intel's EMIB  Samsung Electro-Mechanics is teaming up with Qualcomm to develop "organic bridge" technology for advanced packaging of artificial intelligence (AI) chips. Beyond Qualcomm, Samsung Electro-Mechanics is also co-developing organic bridge technology with several other customers in parallel.  An organic bridge is similar to the silicon bridge used in Intel's EMIB (Embedded Multi-die Interconnect Bridge) packaging, but it uses organic materials, the kind commonly found in printed circuit boards (PCBs), instead of silicon.  According to industry sources on the 14th, Samsung Electro-Mechanics is jointly developing organic bridge technology with Qualcomm for use in 2.1D packaging. Development and validation work has been under way for more than a year. The evaluation involves embedding an organic bridge into a flip-chip ball grid array (FC-BGA) substrate that connects Qualcomm chips, and then assessing the substrate's performance and reliability.  Qualcomm is understood to be considering the organic bridge for large multi-die chips for data centers, among other applications. In October 2024, the company filed a patent for an "Interconnect Bridge" technology, in which a separately fabricated organic material based bridge is embedded in a package substrate to connect multiple dies. In March this year, Qualcomm also hired organic bridge personnel in Korea.  An organic bridge is a component that connects multiple semiconductor dies integrated in a single package. It can be thought of as an ultra small substrate with ultra fine circuitry. It is made by forming multiple layers of fine circuits on an organic dielectric using a redistribution layer (RDL) process. Because it enables finer wiring than the build-up circuitry of a conventional FC-BGA, it can carry more signals across a smaller area. Its role is to raise input/output (I/O) density and bandwidth between dies such as graphics processing units (GPUs) and high bandwidth memory (HBM). It serves a similar function to the silicon bridge used in Intel's EMIB, but it is built on organic material using substrate manufacturing processes rather than semiconductor processes.  Samsung Electro-Mechanics produces the FC-BGA substrate with a recess (cavity) formed at the location where the organic bridge is embedded. For the organic bridge itself, the company is weighing two options: in house production and external sourcing. It produces small quantities of organic bridges for evaluation on its own pilot line, and samples are also made at DSRJ (Device Solutions R&D Japan), the Japanese R&D organization under the Samsung Advanced Institute of Technology (SAIT). The company is also evaluating an approach in which organic bridges manufactured by outside partners are supplied and embedded into its FC-BGA substrates.  In addition to Qualcomm, Samsung Electro-Mechanics is developing organic bridge based 2.1D package substrates with multiple other customers. The strategy is to bypass Intel's EMIB packaging IP. Intel developed EMIB to address the cost and productivity issues of silicon interposers, placing small bridges only where high density connections are required. Intel has applied the technology in mass production since 2017 and has secured the related IP. Several big tech companies are now looking at organic bridges as an alternative to silicon bridges.  Samsung Electro-Mechanics' organic bridge based 2.1D package substrate technology embeds an organic bridge with five to seven redistribution layers (RDL) of circuitry into an FC-BGA. The goal is to shrink the organic bridge's line width and spacing to 1.5 to 2 micrometers (㎛) each, with via sizes of 4 to 5 ㎛. Die to die pattern density is targeted at 500 to 1,000 per millimeter (mm).  Collaboration with Samsung Foundry is also drawing attention. Once Samsung Electro-Mechanics manufactures the organic bridge based FC-BGA, Samsung Foundry evaluates the package's performance and reliability. Samsung Foundry is developing advanced packaging technologies including "2.3D Cube-E," which uses a silicon bridge, and "2.3D Cube-R," which is based on an organic RDL interposer. Samsung Electro-Mechanics' organic bridge substrate is expected to lead to an expansion of Samsung Foundry's packaging services.  $QCOM $INTC | 상대: Intel, ASE | 제품: TPU, HBM | - | 💬 분석가전망 |

Looking at the current state of Samsung Electro-Mechanics' glass substrate work, yields in the initial process of forming holes in the glass through laser processing and etching have risen to 90% or above, but in the subsequent processes of metallizing and filling the holes with copper, yields reportedly drop to around 30%. Solidigm is the U.S. entity SK hynix established when it acquired Intel's NAND flash business at the end of 2021. The total deal size was $9 billion (about 10 trillion won at the exchange rate of the time). Solidigm operates Fab 1 in Dalian, China, and began capital spending on Dalian Fab 2 in the third quarter of this year. In the U.S. it has only its headquarters and an R&D center, with no production base.  The region Solidigm is considering for its first U.S. production base is the East Coast. The company is understood to be discussing the details, including site selection, product mix and the timing of mass production.  A person familiar with the matter said, "Solidigm is pursuing a local mass production line to respond to NAND demand for AI servers from U.S. big tech companies," adding that "it will firm up its investment plan based on long term agreement (LTA) negotiations with customers."  Solidigm has so far focused on developing quad level cell (QLC) NAND for AI data centers. QLC stores four bits in a single cell, the smallest unit of data storage. Compared with other approaches such as triple level cell (TLC), which stores three bits, it is better suited to building high capacity SSDs.  Orders for high capacity NAND have been piling up in particular as the NAND capacity required by next generation AI platforms such as Nvidia's Blackwell and Vera Rubin climbs sharply. According to market research firm TrendForce, NAND revenue at SK hynix (including Solidigm) reached $14.2729 billion (about 19.8 trillion won) in the second quarter of this year, up 89.5% from the previous quarter. Solidigm itself is preparing an initial public offering (IPO) on the Nasdaq.  At an event in New York in July marking the SK hynix ADR listing, Chairman Chey said, "In addition to the advanced packaging facility in Indiana, we are reviewing further investments," and "if conditions such as power, water, workforce and supply chain are in place, building a memory production plant is also possible."  More recently, talk of SK hynix and Intel cooperating on memory production in the U.S. has also surfaced through foreign media and industry sources. It will take time before anything is finalized, but the industry view is that it is feasible, given that U.S. demand for AI memory such as high bandwidth memory (HBM) is expected to keep growing over the medium to long term. This follows its plan to raise demand for "glass carriers," the glass supports that hold wafers while DRAM for HBM is thinned, to 2.5 times this year's level.  According to the semiconductor industry on the 20th, Samsung Electronics will increase its outsourced cleaning volume of glass carriers, which are essential to the HBM production process, from 20,000 units per month this year to 50,000 units per month next year. Only 10,000 glass carriers per month were needed last year, which means the volume doubles this year versus last year and rises 2.5 times next year versus this year.  A glass carrier is a support temporarily attached to the underside of a DRAM wafer for HBM to keep the wafer from warping or cracking while it is ground thin and holes are drilled through it. Because HBM must stack multiple DRAM dies within a limited thickness, wafer thinning technology and warpage control processes become more important as the stack count rises.  HBM4 and HBM4E, for which Samsung Electronics is especially preparing to expand production, center on high stack products of 12-High and above. The industry's analysis is that, even after accounting for the fact that glass carriers are reused after cleaning and that usage varies with how they are deployed at each process step and with yield, a 2.5 times increase in the related volume makes it highly likely that HBM4 and HBM4E production will be at least double this year's level.  In February this year, Samsung Electronics began mass production shipments of HBM4, which applies sixth generation 10nm class (1c) DRAM and a base die built on a 4 nanometer (nm, one billionth of a meter) process. In May, it also provided HBM4E 12-High samples to customers including Nvidia.  The industry expects Samsung Electronics' HBM production scale, measured by monthly average wafer input, to grow nearly 40% from about 180,000 wafers this year to about 250,000 wafers next year. In terms of shipment mix by product, some also forecast that the HBM4 family will rise from around 40% this year to about 80% next year as HBM4E mass production gets into full swing. | **L5 메모리/스토리지** | **삼성전자** (005930 🇰🇷) | 2026-09-23 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Samsung Electronics Revises Roadmap for Next Generation DRAM "D0a," Pulls Hybrid Bonding Forward  Samsung Electronics has revised its next generation DRAM development strategy. The company recently moved "B1b," a technology that had been under development as a standalone project, onto its official roadmap and designated it "D0a," its first DRAM below 10 nanometers (nm).  D0a originally referred to Samsung's vertical channel transistor (VCT) DRAM process. VCT DRAM has now been reclassified under a separate name, "D0a-V." Samsung changed the D0a designation to accelerate the adoption of advanced packaging in commodity DRAM. B1b refers to a technology that uses wafer to wafer (W2W) hybrid bonding to increase internal integration density.  "B1b was in R&D, but it was not on Samsung's official roadmap," said a source familiar with the matter. "With this strategy revision, it has been folded into D0a. It means Samsung is actively pushing for fast commercialization of B1b DRAM."  Samsung Shifts D0a Designation from VCT DRAM to B1b  According to ZDNet Korea's reporting on the 23rd, Samsung has changed the definition of its next generation D0a DRAM from VCT DRAM to B1b.  DRAM has advanced by shrinking internal circuit linewidths. The most recently commercialized DRAM is 1c (sixth generation 10nm class), with linewidths of around 11 to 12nm. The next product, 1d (seventh generation 10nm class), comes in at 10 to 11nm and is effectively the last 10nm class DRAM. These linewidth figures are marketing terms used to indicate performance, not actual physical dimensions.  Samsung has pursued several technological shifts to realize D0a, the first sub 10nm DRAM. B1b is the most prominent of these.  B1b fabricates the memory cells and the surrounding driver circuitry, known as the peri, on separate wafers and then bonds them together, stacking the cell on top of the peri. Compared with the conventional approach of integrating both cell and peri on a single wafer, this allows the cells to be packed more densely. It also lets each of the cell and the peri use a process optimized for it.  B1b uses W2W hybrid bonding. Hybrid bonding joins chips by directly connecting their copper interconnects. Compared with conventional packaging that connects chips through small bumps, it improves integration density and performance. W2W hybrid bonding is also entering full scale adoption in high layer count NAND of 400 layers or more. W2W means bonding one wafer to another.  Why Samsung Is Prioritizing B1b: "VCT DRAM Needs More Time"  VCT DRAM, previously called the D0a process, has been renamed D0a-V as a derivative. VCT stands up the transistor inside the DRAM cell, the smallest unit that stores data, vertically instead of laying it horizontally.  Making the transistor vertical reduces cell area. The conventional cell area, calculated by multiplying the feature size (F) in each direction, is 6F². VCT can bring this down to 4F². A smaller cell means more cells can be integrated in the same area.  VCT DRAM, however, is considered extremely difficult technically. It requires high aspect ratio etching to form vertical transistors and new materials to address the rise in leakage current that comes with smaller cell area.  W2W hybrid bonding, as used in B1b, is already in volume production in existing semiconductor processes such as CMOS image sensors and high layer count NAND. This appears to be why Samsung moved B1b ahead of VCT DRAM in priority for implementing the D0a process.  "From a design standpoint, B1b has a lower barrier to commercialization than VCT, so the industry sees early adoption of B1b as likely," another source said. "Inside Samsung, too, the mood is to move faster on B1b than on VCT."  **B1b to Play a Major Role in Next Generation AI Memory "HBM5"**  B1b could also play a significant role in Samsung's next generation high bandwidth memory (HBM) business. Samsung is currently reviewing applying B1b to HBM5 (eighth generation).  HBM5 is expected to enter mass production around 2029. Samsung is developing the product with a target of raising operating speed by roughly 50% or more over its predecessor, HBM4E.  Commercialization of B1b based D0a DRAM is estimated to be at least two years away. The industry expects the specific mass production investment schedule to be finalized around the middle of next year.  VCT DRAM development is continuing in parallel. The industry view is that VCT DRAM and 3D DRAM could eventually be developed on top of B1b. 3D DRAM stacks the cells themselves vertically and is expected to follow VCT DRAM.  "B1b will ultimately be applied alongside VCT DRAM or 3D DRAM," said Choi Jeongdong, Senior Vice President at TechInsights. "Manufacturing the cell and peri separately brings many advantages, so hybrid bonding will serve as a platform applied together with future DRAM cell architectures." | 상대: ASE | 제품: 2nm GAA, HBM4 | - | ⚠️ 공급망속보 |

First, the wide standard PHY gets replaced with a compact die-to-die link. Samsung shows that its standard HBM4 PHY occupies 8mm x 4mm, while the custom D2D interface needs just 8.5mm x 1.5mm, roughly 60% less area. The better D2D interface also delivers more bandwidth per unit of interface area and simplifies interposer routing. | **L6 광통신/네트워킹** | **Coherent** (COHR 🇺🇸) | 2026-10-01 | [𝕏 Radar] SemiconductorsX(@SemiconductorsX): 摩根士丹利 2026 年 10 月 1 日 美國對中國製光學收發器的限制，最可能從 3.2T 世代開始分階段實施，而非更早的速率。  策略團隊近期與華盛頓資深電信、FCC 及出口管制法律團隊交流後認為，FCC 涵蓋清單（Covered List）是較可能的近期工具，政策目標是降低對中國技術的依賴。限制最快或於 10 月前後啟動，業界已被要求在 3.2T 大規模部署前，提出可信的美國或友岸生產計畫。  即便進入 3.2T，中國製造的收發器仍可能進口，前提是物料清單（BOM）中約 65% 價值來自美國公司；DSP 與雷射大致就能接近這個門檻。因此限制並非全面禁止中國組裝，而是把關鍵高價值零件導向美系供應商。初期 3.2T 出貨預期要到 2029 年才明顯放量，所以即使規則較早落地，對近期營收衝擊有限。  對產業的含義偏向「不卡建置、但卡雷射」：超大規模雲端業者擴產不致出現明顯瓶頸，模組廠也不必被迫接下較低階組裝；但雷射為達到 65% 美國成分門檻，需求仍集中在美系廠商，市場可望維持緊俏。  這對 Lumentum（ $LITE）與 Coherent（ $COHR）偏正面，與本季稍早公司交流一致——任何限制都需分階段、並給出過渡期。後續需觀察磷化銦（InP）基板交貨是否順暢，以免成為實質供應瓶頸。 | - | - | 💬 분석가전망 | `[C2]` |
| **L6 광통신/네트워킹** | **Coherent** (COHR 🇺🇸) | 2026-10-01 | [𝕏 Radar] Alea / Reddit Cur(@aleabitoreddit): "Potential FCC rules on optical transceivers more likely to come at 3.2T" - $LITE, $AAOI, $COHR, $SIVE.  Morgan Stanley met with Washington officials:  - Restrictions would likely start at 3.2T modules made in China (800g/1.6T would be left alone)  - Potential restrictions would likely keep laser market tight (combining US DSPs + lasers could meet the 65% US value threshold per MS)  - Chinese modules could qualify if 65% of bill of materials is US content. Chinese modules using US DSPs and lasers are already near that level  - Analysts expect the positive impact on laser pricing... to be more important than any broad redistribution of transceiver market share.  - Analysts don't expect this to bottleneck hyperscaler AI buildouts, they name InP substrate supply as the key variable to watch (hello $AXTI )   TLDR:  Component suppliers in general are really positive. Like $SMTC probably winner here for TIAs/drivers (I don't think this was listed), maybe $MXL for DSPs too.   $AAOI another large beneficiary.   $SIVE is high-key alpha for laser beneficaries since they have "tremendous capacity" coming online, and that points to US foundries (they haven't formally disclosed location).   Since previous slideshows show 2 US foundries 1 Taiwan foundry (Win Semi) in terms of their capacity partnerships. And it's process of elimination.   From CIOE Channel checks it does look like they're starting to work with Chinese pluggable players, (so they'd avenues moving forward as US supply chain component beneficiaries).   Of course $LITE / $COHR would be happy. | **L7 인프라/특수** | **Apple** (AAPL 🇺🇸) | 2026-09-09 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Micron, Semiconductor Substrate Makers Show Interest in JDI's Mobara Plant Sale  Japanese display maker Japan Display Inc. (JDI) has drawn interest in the sale of its Mobara plant (fab), which halted operations this March, not only from Micron of the United States and several data center operators but also from a semiconductor substrate maker, according to sources on the 8th.  The JDI Mobara plant previously produced sixth generation liquid crystal displays (LCD) and organic light emitting diodes (OLED). It also mass produced OLED panels for the Apple Watch until the second half of 2025. As JDI had announced in the first quarter of 2025, it halted operations at Mobara in March 2026.  Industry source A said, "Micron and several data center operators have submitted letters of intent to JDI to acquire the Mobara plant." Another source B said, "Micron and a Korean semiconductor substrate maker, among others, are in talks with JDI over purchasing the Mobara plant." At its quarterly earnings announcement in early August, JDI stated that it was in discussions with multiple companies regarding the sale of the plant.  The reason Micron, data center operators, and semiconductor substrate makers are interested in the Mobara plant comes down to time. Building a new plant from scratch and securing government permits would take years before mass production could begin. Buying the Mobara plant shortens that timeline accordingly.  Because Mobara previously produced sixth generation LCD and OLED panels, its cleanroom cleanliness levels make it unsuitable for semiconductor front end processing, but the site can be used as a data center or as a back end packaging plant for products such as glass substrates. That is why Micron, data center operators, and substrate makers are interested. The sale price for the plant is currently reported to be under discussion at around KRW 500 billion, though the figure could change depending on circumstances.  OLEDWorks, a US OLED company working with JDI, is reported to be interested not in the Mobara plant but in the 4.5 generation Ishikawa plant. Ishikawa has a line capable of backplane (BP) processing. For OLEDWorks, securing a plant that can reliably handle BP processing going forward is a necessity.  JDI is currently considering selling its plants, including Mobara and Ishikawa, separately to different buyers. If each of these deals goes through, the ownership of JDI's plants would change hands individually.  JDI was formed in 2012 under the lead of the Japanese government (Innovation Network Corporation of Japan) by consolidating the LCD businesses of Sony, Toshiba, and Hitachi. It subsequently failed to respond to market shifts such as the growing adoption of OLED in smartphones and fell behind Korean and Chinese panel makers in competition. In November 2024, JDI unveiled a new catchphrase, "Beyond Display." Whereas the old JDI consisted solely of displays, the company said the new JDI would comprise displays, sensors, artificial intelligence (AI) data centers, and advanced semiconductor packaging.  Meanwhile, most of the equipment related to eLEAP, the technology JDI had developed at the Mobara plant, was purchased by China's HKC. eLEAP patterns red, green, and blue (RGB) OLED subpixels through a photolithography process without using a fine metal mask (FMM). Its viability for mass production has yet to be verified. The technology is similar in principle to FLiPP, which LG Display unveiled at IMID this August.  The appeal of JDI's eLEAP patents is limited. This is because JDI is reported to have sold licenses (rights of use) for the patents to Applied Materials (AMAT), which makes the deposition equipment required to implement eLEAP, and others. JDI sells the licenses to AMAT and others and collects royalties, which means a third party that buys the patents from JDI cannot use them exclusively. Several panel makers in Korea and abroad are developing eLEAP type technology, but there is little incentive to purchase the JDI patents.  $MU | 상대: Meta, Intel | - | ⚠️ 공급망속보 | `[C2]` |
| **L7 인프라/특수** | **Apple** (AAPL 🇺🇸) | 2026-09-10 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Taiwan's Shin Zu Shing supplies only "golden samples" of foldable iPhone hinges to Apple as production remains unstable  Production of the hinge for the iPhone Duo, Apple's first foldable phone unveiled on September 9 (local time), was found on September 10 to remain unstable. The hinge was also the component with the greatest impact on delays to the iPhone Duo production schedule.  The original first vendor for the iPhone Duo hinge was Amphenol of the United States, but Shin Zu Shing, which had been the second vendor, is now producing the hinge as the first vendor. This is because Amphenol's production yield fell far short of expectations.  Industry source A said, "Shin Zu Shing's hinge production yield is still low, and at present only 'golden samples' are being selected and sent to Foxconn." This means that among the hinges Shin Zu Shing makes, only those that meet Apple's standards are being picked out one by one and delivered. Another source, B, said, "Some components are experiencing production disruptions, but the Shin Zu Shing hinge is particularly unstable."  Apple designated multiple vendors for most of the remaining components, excluding items such as the organic light emitting diode (OLED) panel made by Samsung Display. Among iPhone Duo components, the hinge is reportedly the only one for which the first vendor is currently experiencing production disruptions.  Apple applied a "3D printed" hinge module to the iPhone Duo. A hinge module is generally made by combining multiple constituent parts, including gears, shafts, and mechanisms for free stop functionality. A 3D printed hinge module can improve the precision of these constituent parts.  When Apple unveiled the iPhone Duo on September 9, it stated that the hinge contains more than 100 constituent parts. Industry source C said, "Making a foldable phone thinner increases the number of hinge parts," adding, "The hinges in thin foldable phones released by Chinese smartphone makers had 120 to 130 parts." He said, "However, the count differs depending on whether hinge parts are limited to those directly tied to the drive mechanism or whether peripheral parts such as bolts and nuts are included," and added, "We will only know precisely after tearing down the iPhone Duo once it launches."  The change of first vendor is also presumed to be a factor behind Apple's difficulties with hinge production. Typically, the first vendor develops the component together with the set maker and finalizes the specifications. The second vendor is at a disadvantage because it must make the component to specifications that the first vendor has already finalized with the set maker. In effect, Shin Zu Shing is producing hinges to specifications that Amphenol previously finalized with Apple.  Supply of the backplate, another key iPhone Duo component, is also tight. The backplate is the part that supports the foldable panel when it is folded or unfolded. Fine holes are formed in the folding region to secure flexibility and durability. The second vendor for the backplate, China's Lingyi iTech, is reportedly struggling with mass production.  Although production of some iPhone Duo components is still not running smoothly, Apple has reportedly provided suppliers with a component production forecast sufficient to build around 5 million to 6 million finished iPhone Duo units by the end of this year. The number of components exceeds the number of finished units because Apple accounts for yield during final assembly as well as replacement demand from damage after the product is sold.  Apple unveiled the iPhone Duo on September 9, but preorders begin a month later on October 16. The launch date is October 23.  iPhone Duo pricing by storage capacity is $1,999 for 256GB (approximately 2.68 million won at current exchange rates), $2,199 for 512GB (approximately 2.95 million won), $2,599 for 1TB (approximately 3.49 million won), and $3,199 for 2TB (approximately 4.29 million won). Korean launch prices are 3.29 million won for 256GB, 3.59 million won for 512GB, 4.19 million won for 1TB, and 5.09 million won for 2TB.  Source B, quoted above, said, "No iPhone Duo buyer will choose the 256GB model," adding, "The 512GB model is the real 'base model,' and the product price should be viewed as 3 million won or more." By this view, the price of the "base model" (512GB) in the Korean market is 3.6 million won. | 상대: ASE, Samsung | 금액: $1 | ⚠️ 공급망속보 | `[C2]` |

| **L7 인프라/특수** | **SoftBank** (9984 🇯🇵) | 2026-09-14 | [𝕏 Radar] Jukan(@jukan05): Interview with Murata's Vice President on MLCC Market Demand Outlook and Future Strategy (Nikkei)  Q) What is your outlook for the MLCC market?  - MLCC content per AI server is rising, and alongside small size and high capacitance, demand for high heat resistance MLCC is also expanding, driving the product mix toward higher value added  - AI related MLCC demand growth is expected to continue at least through 2028  - Over the medium to long term, demand centered on AI data centers is expected to broaden into edge AI devices  - Physical AI, where robots are controlled by AI, is expected to be a particularly important growth area  - Murata has invested in Noetra, an AI foundation development company backed by more than 40 Japanese companies including SoftBank  - Murata is also participating in related consortia in order to understand where components will be applied and what challenges arise in the mounting process  Q) You plan to invest an additional 80 billion yen in MLCC production equipment over the two years through the fiscal year ending March 2028. What are your plans for further capacity expansion?  - Because Murata prepared ahead of time in anticipation of the spread of autonomous driving, it has secured the factory buildings needed through 2028 ahead of competitors  - Even so, judging by customer demand, MLCC demand is expected to keep expanding beyond 2028  - Murata is reviewing additional capacity expansion plans, including buildings, with a three to five year horizon  - This round of expansion study is bolder than in the past; nothing has been decided yet, but discussions are quite concrete  - Building larger new plant blocks at existing production sites in Japan and overseas is also under consideration as an option  Q) The equity market is paying close attention to MLCC pricing policy. What is your position?  - There is no change to the basic principle that pricing is grounded in long term relationships of trust with customers  - Murata judges that frequently adjusting prices in line with supply and demand conditions could encourage new entrants into the market  - Its position is that pricing decisions that prioritize short term profit do not contribute to raising corporate value over the medium to long term  - That said, if competitors adjust prices substantially, there is a possibility this will affect the product mix and production response Murata is planning on its own  - Accordingly, Murata is monitoring competitors' pricing policies and market trends quite closely  Q) In the 2024 medium term management plan, the policy was to defend share in the volume zone, including commodity and low price products, even at the cost of a lower operating margin. Has that strategy changed since?  - With the production load for high end products rising, an environment has formed in which it is difficult to supply low end products in sufficient volume  - Murata is not abandoning low end market share, but as a result the priority placed on defending that share has fallen somewhat compared with the past  - At the same time, Murata remains on guard against the risk that allowing competitors to enter would let those competitors grow  Q) With the components business, including MLCC, showing standout growth, what is the strategy for the devices business?  - Power modules for AI servers are seen as a major growth opportunity  - Murata plans to allocate capital expenditure and human resources such as engineers aggressively to that area  - For the communications device business, Murata expects revenue and profitability to expand from the fiscal year ending March 2028 onward, using customers' design changes as an opportunity  - Murata is studying in advance how demand for communications devices and sensors will shift with the spread of 6G, the next generation communications standard, and physical AI  - To that end, it is reviewing strategy including not only internal research and development but also the possibility of M&A | - | 기간: through 2028 | 💬 분석가전망 | `[C2]` |

The aim is about 1 terawatt of AI compute a year for Optimus robots, self-driving cars, and space systems.  Phase one is in Grimes County. First spend is $16.8 billion. It has not commented.  Reports also say the Intel side has made limited progress so far.  For Intel, a TSMC path adds pressure on the foundry pitch. It does not erase the existing tie. It queues. A Musk joint plant on advanced nodes would, in this view, help one buyer against the others. That cuts against Morris Chang’s rule of not competing with customers. AMD is in the same question.  He also cites a closed staff meeting when the Intel Terafab report first landed. An employee asked. A senior executive answered with one line: can Musk be trusted?  A second read in the piece: SpaceX has a large post-IPO lockup unlocking in November, and the talks keep the tape hot.  The last warning is the balance sheet. Chang’s memoir says it once turned down a large Apple order and later filled only half, because the horizon mattered more. A big Musk plant means fewer dividends and heavy depreciation. A downturn would hit that. However, I still struggle to understand how he plans to achieve his earlier vision of a fab that largely eliminates the need for conventional cleanrooms without fundamentally redesigning the manufacturing equipment itself.  From what I understand, his team has been requesting quotes from several major semiconductor equipment suppliers.  If he plans to use essentially the same equipment found in today’s fabs, I think particle contamination during equipment maintenance will remain a major challenge.  Even if wafers are completely isolated during transportation, what happens when the equipment needs to be opened for maintenance? Without fundamentally changing how these tools are designed and serviced, I have a hard time understanding how his fab would be significantly different from conventional fabs.  Or perhaps his plan is to follow a relatively conventional approach through the expected semiconductor supply shortage around 2028, while developing something fundamentally different for the longer term.
